Micron Technology vs. Qualcomm: Which Tech Stock Is the Better Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One of these chipmakers is riding a record AI-driven surge, while the other is working to reinvent itself beyond the smartphone.

Semiconductors power everything from smartphones to massive artificial intelligence (AI) servers. Choosing between high-growth memory leaders and diversified wireless giants like Micron Technology (MU -2.52%) and QUALCOMM (QCOM -2.64%) is a pivotal decision for investors today.

Micron Technology focuses on the memory and storage components essential for modern computing. Qualcomm provides the processors and modems that connect the world. Comparing these semiconductor leaders helps clarify where the next wave of growth might originate as we look toward 2027.

Micron Technology specializes in memory and storage solutions, primarily manufacturing DRAM and NAND chips for smartphones and artificial intelligence servers. The company now generates approximately 60% of its revenue from the data center market. Because its top ten customers account for over half of its total revenue, customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 3, 2026, revenue reached nearly $133 billion. This represents a significant revenue growth of approximately 256% compared to the prior year. The company reported a net income of roughly $85 billion during this period, driven by high demand for high-bandwidth memory products.

As of its September 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is 0x. This ratio measures total debt, including both short- and long-term obligations, against shareholders' equity, and a value of zero suggests the company has negligible debt. The current ratio of 3.3x indicates ample assets to cover short-term liabilities, and it generated nearly $59.0 billion in free cash flow in the fiscal year ended Sept. 3, 2026.

Qualcomm designs the modems and processors that power millions of mobile devices while diversifying into the automotive and Internet of Things markets. Apple and Samsung are major customers, with each representing 10% or more of total revenues. This reliance on large partners means Qualcomm remains a central player in the semiconductor industry.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 27, 2026, revenue reached nearly $43 billion. This was a slight decline, as revenue growth was approximately- 2.6% over the previous year. Despite the dip in sales, the company achieved a net income of roughly $13.6 billion, aided by its high-margin patent licensing business.

As of its September 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.5x, reflecting a moderate use of total debt relative to shareholder equity. The current ratio of 1.9x indicates it can comfortably cover short-term liabilities. In the fiscal year ended Sept. 27, 2026, free cash flow was nearly $8.7 billion.

Micron Technology faces significant volatility in chip prices, leading to unpredictable revenue cycles. It deals with intense competition in the data center market and regulatory challenges stemming from restrictions imposed on its products by the Chinese government. The company must also manage the costs of massive expansion projects while competing against rivals like Samsung and SK Hynix.

Qualcomm faces high revenue concentration from mobile handset makers, who are increasingly seeking to develop their own internal modems. This shift toward vertical integration, especially by customers like Apple, could reduce future demand for its connectivity products. The company also faces substantial exposure to trade tensions in China and ongoing legal challenges regarding its patent licensing practices.

Investors must choose between the lower Forward P/E based on future earnings estimates and the lower P/S ratio, which measures market value against sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

The company is expanding into the data center market, targeting $5 billion in revenue by fiscal 2027 and $15 billion in data center revenue by fiscal 2029. It is also seeing rapid growth in its automotive segment.

Moreover, Qualcomm is not dependent on growing investment in new data centers. It is aiming to gain share of existing spending. This makes it a potentially safer stock to ride the data center boom, especially given the prospect of additional guardrails on new data center construction following the midterm elections.

Micron is too sensitive to memory pricing for me to be comfortable holding the stock beyond 2027. It might continue to hit new highs over the next 12 months, but in the longer term, I think Qualcomm has a more durable business that can still deliver market-beating returns to shareholders in 2027 and through the end of the decade.

10 Reasons for the Big Blue Wave

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I wrote the other day about a top reason US voters said they voted for Democrats in such large numbers — corruption in the Trump household, Trump administration, and Republican Party. It was surprising to me that this showed up as the biggest factor for so many people … even though it was also a huge one for me. However, I think everyone knows what the most significant reason was for the dramatic, historic shift in voting. It’s the economy, stupid! That’s the famous old quote from James Carville, a political strategist working for Bill Clinton when he was running for president in 1992, and it’s as relevant as ever. With that in mind, let’s take a look at some of the top reasons for the Big Blue Wave.

Inflation has hit and hit and hit. Donald Trump said while campaigning for president in 2024, “Starting on day one, we will end inflation and make America affordable again.” However, of course, beyond the fact that he didn’t come close to doing that and was just making an obviously false promise, Trump actually jacked up inflation significantly with his obsessive, high tariffs and with his war on Iran. Whereas it’s often hard to tie inflation to a specific presidential policy, in this case, we had two specific presidential policies that clearly caused inflation! Even when there are no clear links, voters blame the president for such matters, so imagine how much they must have done so in this case!

Aside from inflation, the economy overall isn’t great. The country isn’t adding a bunch of jobs, wages and salaries aren’t rising much, and people by and large don’t feel optimistic about their financial futures. So, combined with quickly rising costs, a very meh economy had voters wanting change.

As already discussed at length, voters were fed up with corruption in the Republican Party. As some CleanTechnica readers pointed out, if people were prospering financially and not being hit with severe inflation, many of them probably wouldn’t care about the corruption. However, when considering #1 and #2, people naturally get quite frustrated with blatant corruption and essentially high-volume robbery from Donald Trump, the Trump family, Trump staffers, and the Republican Party broadly.

Naturally, this is heavily linked to #1, since it is largely this war that has led to gas prices rising so much. However, also more broadly, Americans are fed up with forever wars in the Middle East, there’s never been a good and strong case for why Trump started this war (as tends to be the case with these wars in the Middle East), and many people really don’t like seeing innocent schoolchildren being bombed to death. Trump was supposed to be an anti-war president (I know that many people believed that, even though Trump is a naturally aggressive, violent person who seemed to me like someone who would obviously get into wars). Voters who cared a lot about this topic might have felt betrayed by Trump and swung strongly away from him and the Republican Party (which has a tendency to get us into these oil wars in the Middle East).

Even for many people who thought we needed stronger actions to stop people from coming to the United States, the inhumane, deadly, Gestapo-like ICE army — often including violent people with their faces covered and without ID acting like the mafia — was a step or ten too far. There are countless stories of good, innocent people being jailed without cause, or even murdered, by this Nazi-like police force. There was clearly a huge blue shift in certain places as a result of this.

Many voters have gotten extremely concerned about Republicans under Trump’s leadership eroding the foundations of our democracy. While this became a major problem in Trump’s first term, he has gone much further in his second term. Apparently, more people have woken up to this problem and it has freaked them out.

A massive rollout of AI and data centers — often feeling completely forced on us — has upset a lot of Americans. This does come back to #1, too. Giant data centers have jacked up electricity prices a ton in several regions. Others are threatened with the same thing happening. The same thing has also happened from the crypto craze, which more and more people have also found to be a scam. Seeing billions upon billions trillions of dollars funneled toward AI companies while people are struggling, and then seeing electricity prices rising significantly — surprise, surprise — pisses people off and makes them vote for change.

No matter how you cut it, people vote based on money — in particular, their own money. Whether it’s food prices, gas prices, electricity prices, clothing prices, doll prices, computer prices, car prices, or other prices, people don’t like it when they have to pay a lot more money for stuff — especially when they aren’t making much more money, or more money at all. Simply put, this matter deserves extra weight, because the critical thing swing voters are often voting on is just that — money.

Of course, there are solar, wind, and EV aspects to all of this. Trump has made the US spend a fortune to stop wind and solar farms from providing the US with cheap electricity, and he has made the US spend a fortune keeping old, dirty coal power plants open. High gas prices have also pushed people to buy electric cars, but mostly in other countries where there’s more EV choice and more people have more sense on this topic. However, by and large, I don’t think American voters really shifted much based on Trump stopping renewable energy projects, promoting coal and gas, or limiting EV choice in the United States. People voting for Democrats on climate and cleantech matters were already voting for Democrats based on these issues. It’s unlikely many people at all shifted from red to blue because of these factors.

Zach is tryin' to help society help itself one word at a time. He spends most of his time here on CleanTechnica as its editor-in-chief and CEO. Zach is recognized globally as an electric vehicle, solar energy, and energy storage expert. He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

Dutch Bros vs. Wendy's: Which Stock Is a Better Buy?

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Dutch Bros is adding drive-thru shops at a rapid clip, while Wendy's relies on a franchise model built for steady cash flow. Their valuations tell very different stories.

Both Dutch Bros (BROS +6.50%) and Wendy's (WEN +1.22%) are different types of restaurant stocks, but each has the same challenge of creating more shareholder value. Dutch Bros is trying to build its future, while Wendy's is trying to improve its present.

Dutch Bros focuses on high-speed beverage service with a loyal following, while Wendy's leans on a massive global franchise network to deliver consistent results. Both compete for consumer dollars in a crowded market, but they offer very different risk and reward profiles for long-term investors.

Dutch Bros operates a rapidly growing network of drive-thru shops specializing in coffee, teas, and customizable energy drinks. In the competitive world of retail stocks, it relies on a unique culture and digital rewards to drive loyalty, particularly among younger consumers. The company sources its proprietary Rebel energy drinks through third-party partners.

In FY 2025, revenue reached nearly $1.6 billion, representing a significant increase of approximately 27.9% over the prior year. This growth is largely driven by the opening of new locations across the United States. The company also reported net income of roughly $117.3 million, showing a substantial rise from the previous fiscal period.

Wendy's operates a global quick-service restaurant system with over 7,000 locations. The business uses a system optimization strategy involving buying and selling restaurant units to maintain an efficient mix of company-owned and franchised stores.

In FY 2025, revenue reached approximately $2.2 billion, which was a decrease of roughly 3.1% compared to the previous year. Despite the lower top-line figure, the company produced net income of nearly $165.1 million. This resulted in a net margin of close to 7.6%, a figure showing how much of every dollar in revenue becomes profit after all expenses.

Dutch Bros faces risks from its geographic concentration in the Western United States, making it vulnerable to regional economic shifts. The business also relies on a small group of suppliers for key ingredients, which could lead to disruptions if those partners fail to deliver. Additionally, rapid store expansion requires flawless execution, and any failure to manage this growth could harm the brand reputation.

Wendy's operates in the fiercely competitive quick-service industry against other major chains and convenience outlets. The company carries significant leverage through its securitized financing facility, which requires consistent cash flow to service. It also depends heavily on independent franchisees to maintain standards, as any food safety incident can damage the global brand.

Wendy's has a lower forward price-to-earnings (P/E) using future earnings estimates; Dutch Bros carries a higher price-to-sales (P/S) ratio measuring market cap against sales over the past twelve months.

When comparing two stocks, it really comes down to which one will create more value for a shareholder, and each of these stocks has its own set of risks and rewards.

The risks and rewards with Dutch Bros largely come down to its expansion plans. It's opening new locations rapidly, opening 48 stores in the third quarter of 2026, bringing it to over 1,200 locations. It hopes to reach over 2,000 stores by 2029. But it's not in every state, which limits its brand recognition. In its third quarter, it also shared that as a percentage of company-operated shops revenues, occupancy costs increased by 100 basis points, as the company shifted more of its portfolio to build-to-suit leases. Higher utility and other operating expenses also played a role. That's worth paying attention to because higher rent and utility costs could weigh on the profitability of new locations compared to some of its legacy locations. That means, while expansion is one of the biggest opportunities for the company, that expansion could also be one of the biggest risks, as new locations may cost more to open and operate and be less successful than legacy locations.

For Wendy's, it's a company that's already shown its potential, but it's at a stage where it's largely focused on improving what it's built. For the first nine months of 2026, net income is down 49.2%. Still, there's promise in its breakfast offerings, which management says could unlock "up to $200 million in restaurant-level EBITDA across the system." In addition, while shareholders wait for the turnaround to take shape, Wendy's pays a dividend that yields a generous 3.4%.

Choosing between these two boils down to believing in Dutch Bros' growth plan or the improvement plan of Wendy's.

7 Action Shows Better Than 'The Terminal List'

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One of the most dominant genres on television is the action thriller. Action shows are popular because they deliver universal, high-energy entertainment that triggers a natural surge of excitement and escapism. With every network and streaming getting in on the thrilling game, it's opening up a world of endless options that will keep your adrenaline pumping from start to finish. One such series is The Terminal List. Now in its second season, the Prime Video series, based on the Jack Carr novel, follows Chris Pratt as James Reece, a U.S. Navy SEAL who uncovers a military and government conspiracy.

A high-octane show, The Terminal List scratches the itch for most action lovers. But if it’s not quite up to par with your expectations, there are seven action shows that are considered better. From a classic early 2000s thriller to masterful page-to-screen adaptations, the seven titles on this list fulfill the action quota you found in The Terminal List but amplify it in more profound and exciting ways. While it likely comes down to taste, what’s clear is these shows deliver the thrills you desire.

The early 2000s world of action was defined by the signature Fox series 24. The ticking-clock political thriller created by Joel Surnow and Robert Cochran follows counter-terrorism agent Jack Bauer (Kiefer Sutherland) as he races against the clock to stop global threats. Bauer must navigate high-stakes political conspiracies, assassination attempts, nuclear plots, and deep ethical compromises to protect national security. Each season unfolds in a unique "real-time" format, where every one-hour episode represents one hour of a single, chaotic 24-hour day. 24 dazzled viewers for nine seasons, plus a movie and a single-season revival, thanks to its high-pressure, real-time format, relentless narrative momentum, and top-tier action hero lead.

Whether you watched or not, 24 is defined by its iconic digital countdown clock and beep audio signal commercial breaks, building constant anxiety and urgency. From there, Sutherland was in charge. Bauer takes an "ends justify the means" approach: lying, torturing, and sacrificing his personal life to stop catastrophic threats. The show popularized a multi-panel split-screen technique that tracked simultaneous events across different locations and made phone calls or operational command rooms feel dynamic and fast-paced. Tapping into real-world anxieties surrounding terrorism, national security, and presidential politics as it premiered just after 9/11, 24 turned counter-terrorism operations into a high-octane spectacle. While both shows examine drastically different eras of action, 24 has broader stakes compared to the one-man vendetta machine. While The Terminal List prides itself on hyper-specific military hardware and Tier-1 operator accuracy, 24 focuses heavily on psychological pressure, interrogation standoffs, and the physical toll taken on its lead character. 24 pioneered the modern television event, opening doors for shows like The Terminal List.

If there's one show that proves women can be just as incredible in the action genre, it's Lioness. Created by Taylor Sheridan, the espionage thriller, also known as Special Ops: Lioness, follows Joe McNamara (Zoe Saldaña), a CIA station chief who manages a covert military program deploying female undercover operatives to infiltrate international terrorist networks. Throughout their missions, both the handlers and the undercover assets must navigate extreme psychological pressure and intense personal sacrifices. The show centers on these elite operatives as they establish close relationships with high-target individuals to dismantle dangerous organizations from within. The compelling, high-stakes action thriller brings gritty realism, high-voltage tactical sequences, and a deep emotional toll.

Whether tracking targets across international hotspots like the Middle East or Europe, or plunging into intense tactical firefights, the pacing keeps the tension tightly wound. Lioness shines for its practical stunts, realistic gunfights, and grounded military maneuvers that give the combat sequences a heavy, visceral pulse. Saldaña is exceptional, leading an extraordinary ensemble that also features Laysla De Oliveira, Nicole Kidman, Michael Kelly, and Morgan Freeman. With a heavyweight supporting cast, Lioness adds institutional and political gravity to the chain of command. The series interweaves the adrenaline of black-ops intelligence work with the quiet, destabilizing strain it places on family life. If you prefer an ensemble-driven series, Lioness is Sheridan doing what he does best. With a bigger cinematic scale, Lioness has a blockbuster sensibility that moves beyond a straightforward, black-and-white revenge quest.

It's safe to say that Reacher is the official king of modern action television. Based on the Lee Child novel series, Reacher follows Jack Reacher (Alan Ritchson), a massive, highly intelligent former U.S. Army military police investigator who lives as a nomadic drifter. As he travels across the United States with nothing but the clothes on his back, he is continuously pulled into deadly conspiracies and criminal rings. Driven by a strict personal moral code, he uses his formidable physical strength and sharp analytical skills to dismantle corruption and deliver aggressive street justice. Reacher stands at the top of the heap as it leans into old-school pulp thrills, pairs a physically faithful lead with smart investigative instincts, and delivers grounded, visceral set pieces.

Reacher takes the ideals of an action blockbuster and gives it the room to breathe. It lets allies like Oscar Finlay (Malcolm Goodwin), Roscoe Conklin (Willa Fitzgerald), and Frances Neagley (Maria Sten) develop properly as the narrative builds organic tension through a multi-episode mystery. Ritchson carries the weight of the world, matching Child’s literary description of the titular character. He combines an imposing, human-wrecking-ball presence with a deadpan, quiet confidence and sharp dry wit. Ritchson performs many of his own stunts, giving the action a tangible feel. Reacher versus The Terminal List is a battle between pulp fun and grim realism. Ritchson's hulking Sherlock Holmes gives Reacher brilliant depth, while Pratt brings trauma, physical degradation, and a darker, more obsessive mindset. Reacher is a more straightforward drama with street justice, corruption-busting, and self-contained town mysteries, while The Terminal List is a bit denser. If you prefer a classically tinged action adventure, Reacher is most certainly for you.

Based on the Frederick Forsyth novel and 1973 film of the same name, the Peacock iteration of The Day of the Jackal is peak spy thriller. Created by Ronan Bennett, The Day of the Jackal follows an elusive, cold-blooded contract assassin, code name Jackie (Eddie Redmayne), who carries out high-profile hits for astronomical fees. After a high-risk assignment draws the attention of Bianca Pullman (Lashana Lynch), a tenacious British intelligence officer, a destructive, international cat-and-mouse chase ensues across Europe. As both professionals risk their personal lives to outsmart each other, global forces pull strings from the shadows. The series works as a gripping action-thriller because it takes the novel's sharp mechanics and expands them into a high-stakes, modern cat-and-mouse game.

The 10-episode series gives the narrative room to explore how a high-end assassin operates and survives in an era dominated by digital tracking, global intelligence networks, and modern surveillance. It's a tour-de-force acting phenomenon. Redmayne plays the Jackal as an icy, calculating chameleon, going completely against type. He's the antagonist, and yet watching him succeed is half the fun. The show treats his process like an actor's playground, focusing on prosthetic disguises, accent changes, operational craft, and psychological precision, giving Redmayne room to showcase his vast skill set. Lynch matches with more heroic tenacity, obsession, and ruthlessness. Both characters operate in a morally gray world where their professional drives relentlessly clash with and threaten their personal lives. The latter more so in devastating fashion. Filmed across international locations like London, Budapest, Vienna, and Croatia, the series delivers tightly choreographed sniper hits, tense standoffs, and explosive action sequences. The two series are quite different, highlighting a grounded, cat-and-mouse European espionage thriller and a high-octane military-revenge action saga. While The Terminal List focuses on Reece, The Day of the Jackal champions the antagonist. This villain-centric perspective creates a darker, morally ambiguous tone, and sometimes that's a juicier watch. ​​​​​​​

One of the most-watched Netflix series of the 2020s is the political action thriller The Night Agent. Created by Shawn Ryan and based on the novel of the same name by Matthew Quirk, The Night Agent follows Peter Sutherland (Gabriel Basso), a low-level FBI agent working in the White House basement. When an emergency phone he monitors finally rings, he is thrust into a dangerous, high-stakes conspiracy reaching the highest levels of the U.S. government. Alongside a cybersecurity expert named Rose Larkin (Luciane Buchanan), he must race against time to uncover a deadly mole and protect American power. A thoroughly engaging thrill ride, The Night Agent kicks off with an irresistible hook and unrelenting action that never slows down.

The Night Agent is built around tight, bingeworthy episodes; almost every installment ends with a suspenseful twist, new evidence, or a cliffhanger that drives you straight into the next hour. It features practical stunts, intense shootouts, hand-to-hand combat, and nerve-wracking chases without getting bogged down by overly dense or confusing mechanics. The narrative scales from a localized murder mystery into a sprawling political conspiracy threatening the Oval Office and national security, keeping the tension high across its multi-season run. The two shows approach action differently: propulsive, high-concept political paranoia versus gritty, military-authentic revenge. The Night Agent thrives as a pulpy thriller, driven by rapid action and instant gratification. The narrative moves briskly from a basement White House emergency phone line straight into a sprawling government conspiracy. Peter emerges as a beloved hero that you instantly cheer on. The Terminal List tightly centers on a singular, visceral path of personal retribution of a man who isn't as likable as Peter. ​​​​​​​

Even if you know the character from the comics, The Punisher blends Marvel ideals with bone-crunching action into a brazen two-season thriller. The Steve Lightfoot-created series follows former Marine Frank Castle (Jon Bernthal) as he tracks down those responsible for the murder of his family. After avenging his loved ones, Castle uncovers a deep military and government conspiracy that forces him to operate as a brutal, lethal vigilante while teaming up with allies to take out his rivals. The Punisher is a character-driven drama because it grounds its brutal spectacle in raw human grief, moral grey areas, and visceral realism. Unlike traditional comic book shows with mystical enemies or high-tech lasers, the street-level action is gritty, messy, and focused on hand-to-hand combat, gunplay, and sheer endurance.

Frank operates with a brutal, no-compromise efficiency that sets him apart from heroes who rely on mercy or repeated incarceration. Bernthal embodies Frank Castle as a man constantly on edge, consumed by rage, pain, and exhaustion. The story treats Castle's existence as a tragedy, showing the heavy toll of war and loss. What allows the series to stand out is its brilliant approach to pertinent themes revolving around post-traumatic stress disorder, the struggles of returning military veterans, and institutional hypocrisy. Like The Terminal List, this story is about a one-man vengeance machine. The difference is the emotional depth that Bernthal brings compared to Pratt. The Punisher explores the grim reality of unchecked vigilantism, military conspiracies, and the ethics of lethal force within a flawed justice system. By contrast, The Terminal List is more of a straightforward, single-minded anti-hero revenge trip where Reece systematically clears a list of targets. Plus, the first-season dynamic between Bernthal and his The Bear costar, Ebon Moss-Bachrach, is worth the watch.

If there's one character who's emerged as a timeless action hero, it's Jack Ryan. Played by a bevy of stars over the years, the latest iteration has many fans noting it’s the best. In Tom Clancy's Jack Ryan, CIA analyst Jack Ryan (John Krasinski) is pulled from his desk job into high-stakes field operations after discovering suspicious bank transfers. As he tracks a rising terrorist figurehead across Europe and the Middle East, he evolves into a field operative, uncovering global conspiracies and internal corruption. Based on the iconic book series, Tom Clancy's Jack Ryan blends high-stakes geopolitical scope with an everyman hero who relies on brainpower as much as brawn.

Tom Clancy's Jack Ryan weaves through realistic financial tracking, intelligence tradecraft, and moral gray areas, grounding the story in stellar geopolitical narratives. With a massive episodic budget, the series delivers movie-tier action sequences, globetrotting locations and tense tactical set pieces. Krasinski portrays Jack Ryan as a vulnerable, brainy CIA financial analyst who gets dragged out of a desk job and has to learn and adapt on the fly. By giving him an everyman essence, he becomes a highly accessible character. The dynamic, organic bromance and working partnership between Jack Ryan and his mentor/boss James Greer (Wendell Pierce) add levity to the weighty action. If you're seeking cinematic-level action, Tom Clancy's Jack Ryan is for you. It's a much brainier action series that maintains a bit more structural optimism, with Ryan working within and trying to fix or protect larger institutional frameworks even when bad actors infiltrate them. Over its four seasons, Tom Clancy's Jack Ryan offers more variety. While both shows feature two standout sitcom stars, it's Krasinski's transition to an action hero that is more enthralling to watch.

Gaine Expands Consent and Preference Management Solution Across Healthcare Markets

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Building on proven success in pharmaceutical and life sciences, Gaine introduces enterprise-wide consent and preference management for healthcare payers and providers ahead of HLTH 2026.

SAN LUIS OBISPO, Calif., Nov. 13, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Gaine Technology, a leader in healthcare data management, today announced the expansion of its Consent and Preference Management solution across healthcare markets, building on its established success in the pharmaceutical and life sciences industry.

With more than 50 million patient consents under management, Gaine is extending its proven capabilities to address the growing need among health plans and healthcare providers to manage consent, communication preferences, and data-sharing permissions across increasingly complex and fragmented technology environments.

As healthcare organizations accelerate interoperability, digital engagement, and AI initiatives, the ability to consistently identify individuals, understand their preferences, and honor consent decisions across systems has become increasingly critical.

"Healthcare organizations have invested heavily in capturing consent, but the real challenge is ensuring those decisions follow the individual across systems, programs, and interactions," said Martin Dunn, CEO of Gaine Technology. "Building on our success in life sciences, we're expanding these capabilities to help payers and providers establish a trusted, enterprise-wide approach to consent and preference management."

Moving Beyond Traditional Consent Management

Built on Gaine's Health Data Management Platform (HDMP), the solution goes beyond traditional consent capture by connecting consent and preferences to trusted identities, relationships, programs, communication channels, and applicable data-sharing rules.

Unlike standalone consent applications, Gaine does not require organizations to replace existing systems. Instead, HDMP serves as an enterprise data management and orchestration layer, helping ensure individual choices are consistently understood and applied across the organization.

Expanding a Proven Healthcare Data Foundation

Gaine's expansion brings established life sciences capabilities into the payer and provider markets, addressing use cases spanning member engagement, care management, patient communications, data sharing, and enterprise governance.

With a healthcare-specific ontology refined over more than 15 years, Gaine provides the identity, context, and relationships needed to transform fragmented consent records into trusted, actionable information.

10 Best Sci-Fi Reads Better Than Black Mirror

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Charlie Brooker's Black Mirror is technically science fiction, but it's so much more than that. It shines a light on our society and technology in a way that's disturbingly relatable. Watching it feels uncomfortable because, as unsettling as the stories it portrays are, sometimes they're not too far off from the lives we actually live.

If you've binged the entire show and are still looking for more content with the same vibes, there are many science fiction books that feel like reading a Black Mirror episode. They coat social commentary in a tense and unsettling atmosphere. After reading these, you might come away understanding society a bit better than when you started... for better or worse.

Every day, there's an increasingly large number of mouths to feed. In Tender Is the Flesh by Agustina Bazterrica (available for purchase here), the problem becomes even worse when a vius makes all animal meat poisonous to humans.

The solution that the government in the novel comes up with is raising humans as livestock. The protagonist, Marcos, works at a processing plant for this "special meat." When he's gifted a live woman for his consumption, he starts to warm up to her and begins to do the unthinkable: Treat her like a human being.

We live at a time when human beings are increasingly looking to artificial intelligence for companionship. Annie Bot by Sierra Greer (available for purchase here) gives a fresh take to the concept of the perfect computer-powered girlfriend, which has been seen in media and movies plenty of times.

The story is told from the point of view of Annie Bot, the AI woman who was created to be the perfect companion for Doug. She sees to his every whim, keeps the house clean, and tries to give him what he wants.

But the more human she becomes, the less perfect she gets. Slowly, it becomes apparent that Doug might not really know what he even wants, and that Annie Bot might be able to look beyond her role as companion.

Everyday things have been disappearing in The Memory Police by Yoko Ogawa (available for purchase here). It starts with small things, like hats, roses, birds. These objects don't just vanish from the world, but even from people's memories, like they never even existed in the first place.

The protagonist is a young woman who's one of the few with the power to remember the lost objects. This puts her at risk from the overbearing Memory Police, who exist to make sure that the forgotten remains forgotten, at any cost.

Little by little, more serious things start to disappear with horrible repercussions. All the protagonist can do is watch as life changes completely, while everyone around her remains oblivious that anything wrong is even happening: a situation that would feel right at home on Black Mirror.

When considering how technology interacts with society, it's impossible not to mention the classic Do Androids Dream Of Electric Sheep? by Philip K Dick (available for purchase here). This sci-fi novel inspired the Blade Runner series and remains one of the most beloved sci-fi novels.

The book takes place in a post-apocalyptic world that's shrouded in a cloud of radioactive dust, and most of humanity has escaped to Mars. Rick Deckard remains on Earth with his pet electric sheep to do a job: He's an android killer who tracks down and kills rogue robots for the police.

His latest assignment is to track and destroy six androids on the run from authorities. The only problem is that these androids are very smart, and they look and sound exactly like regular people.

There Is No Antimemetics Division by an author known only as qntm (available for purchase here) is an SCP novel. If you're unfamiliar, the fictional SCP Foundation secures, contains, and protects anomalies and strange supernatural phenomena and entities.

The titular Antimemetics Division is a branch of the SCP that deals with anti-memes, or ideas that can't be remembered or spread. These entities mess with people's minds and prevents them from remembering or sharing information about them, obviously making it extremely difficult to trap or contain them.

The story is told over the course of several seemingly unconnected short stories. Eventually, you realize two things: everything is connected, and there's a horrible threat looming over humanity, but no one can remember that it exists. This is a strange and unsettling read that comes together in a very satisfying way and explores the way human beings think and act, much like an episode of Black Mirror.

Many of the novels by John Marrs are Black Mirror-esque and explore a world where technology and science are just a little too far advanced for its own good. The sci-fi novel The One (available for purchase here) is the first entry in the Dark Future collection, and a great place to start if you're looking for your next realistic dystopian fix.

In The One, a new service has rolled out genetic matchmaking. A new DNA test can match you to your perfect partner, who you're the most genetically compatible with. The book follows five people from all walks of life who find out that being matched to their soul mates might not be all it's cracked up to be.

Blake Crouch is a much more action-focused sci-fi novel than many of the others on this list, but Dark Matter (available for purchase here) still feels very much like a story straight from Black Mirror. Jason Dessen is a physics professor on his way home when he's knocked out by a mysterious person. When he wakes up, he discovers that his life is completely different.

His wife isn't married to him, his son doesn't exist, and his work is a lot more significant than his teaching was. In this world, Jason has done something impossible, but he doesn't want the fame and status. All he wants is to go back home to his wife and son. This sends him on an incredible journey, pursued by an enemy that might be indestructible.

The Test by Sylvain Neuvel (available for purchase here) is a short read, but it's one that will stay on your mind long after you've finished it. Written by one of the contributing writers for Black Mirror, The Test asks what it takes to be a good contributing member of society.

The book's protagonist, Idir, is facing the British Citizenship Test: a brief questionnaire of just 25 multiple-choice questions. It begins simple enough, with a few easy questions. But before long, the testing descends into a dystopian life or death situation that will make you question how you value life and what makes a good citizen.

Exhalation by Ted Chiang (available for purchase here) is an anthology consisting of nine short stories. Each tale has the dreamy, slightly "off" feel of a Black Mirror episode, and scenarios that feel like you get not one but nine whole new episodes.

The collection explores moral and philosophical questions. If you had the option to go back in time and fix some mistakes, would you? How would multiple universes change the way we see free will and choice? These profound and speculative stories will make you reexamine what you think about the world and human nature.

The movie version of Kazuo Ishiguro's Klara and the Sun—a story about a solar-poweed, child-like companion android—has recently brought the author's dream-like writing to the public eye. Never Let Me Go (available for purchase here) is another story worth checking out for fans of Black Mirror.

In this book, we spend some time learning about Kathy, a 31-year-old woman with fond memories of her exclusive private school. As she remembers her life as a student, the memories are tinged with nostalgia and an air of unease.

There's something very wrong about the school, that has unsettling ramifications for Kathy's future. If you're a fan of the eerie atmosphere of Black Mirror, this one might be just right for you.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Why New Boeing Planes Are Green Before Delivery, But Airbus Aircraft Aren't

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The observation that pre-delivery Boeing aircraft are green, and Airbus aluminum aircraft aren't, is partially true, but it is far from the full picture. There are significant differences in the substances that Boeing and Airbus' various divisions and subcontractors use, and this is partially driven by environmental regulations differing by location. But unfinished Boeing aircraft are also typically photographed at an earlier stage of development compared with Airbus.

After the peelable Temporary Protective Coating (TPC / "Spraylat") is removed, Boeing aircraft look different, but they are rarely photographed at that stage. This is the stage that Airbus aircraft are typically photographed. Importantly, the 'green' conversation requires aluminum aircraft, as composite material structures are treated differently. Here is what to know about Boeing and Airbus coatings.

One of the most distinctive features of new aircraft is the stage they go through when they are in their primary coats of paint. Boeing aircraft are green as they roll out of the assembly facility. The green coating is a corrosion-protective primer applied to the aircraft structure before its final exterior paint scheme. The layer also helps subsequent coatings to adhere to the aircraft. Other aircraft manufacturers such as Airbus, Embraer, ATR, and COMAC use broadly comparable surface-protection coatings, although they are not quite the same.

It is also important to distinguish between the aircraft's three basic stages of coating. Aircraft receive structural primer, intermediate coating, and final decorative topcoats. What looks like a single coat of green paint on Boeing aircraft is actually a combination of two distinct coatings, obviously not the final decorative one. What is seen is typically the Temporary Protective Coating (TPC / "Spraylat") and the Corrosion-Inhibiting Primer (The True Undercoat).

This does not apply to the composite-material-constructed 787 Dreamliner (more on that below). Instead, it is applied to traditional aluminum structures. It is worth noting that while the Boeing 737 is now in its fourth (MAX) generation, its fuselage remains a fundamentally aluminum structure and a 1960s design. Something similar is also true of the heavily updated Boeing 777X soon to enter service. It may come with new wings, but its fuselage remains primarily aluminum.

Boeing's clean-sheet Boeing 787 is its only 'new' aircraft in the narrow sense of being a clean-sheet design able to take full advantage of today's advanced composite materials. The only 'new' Airbus analog is the Airbus A350. The A330neo and A320neo series are upgraded older aluminum fuselage aircraft. The Boeing 787's fuselage and wings are built with carbon-fiber-reinforced polymer (CFRP) rather than aluminum and so do not suffer from galvanic or atmospheric metal corrosion.

Consequently, Dreamliners do not come with green TPC or green chromate primers. As they roll out of the assembly line, they appear white or light-gray/beige, being coated with conductive/UV-protective primer coats. The Dreamliner's primary competitor, the A350, comes with a tan/beige primer coating, which serves a similar function. Sections in white or beige are usually composite materials, which don't corrode and don't need the coating. That's why the composite-bodied 787 looks less green than the aluminum 737.

Stage 1: component fabrication, sub-assembly transit

Stage 2: Final assembly, outdoor flight line

Bare bright silver or matte pale yellow-green

This is perhaps the most striking on the Boeing 777X. As stated, it retained the 1990s-era aluminum fuselage. Accordingly, the fuselage is painted green in the signature glossy emerald-green Temporary Protective Coating (TPC). Meanwhile, the wings are made of composite and so receive a white or light-gray UV-protective topcoat that matches the Boeing 787. Boeing has also applied a strippable green film to its early-built 777-9s, which have been stored outdoors awaiting certification for years.

A closer look at the paint and production processes of Boeing airliners.

Airbus' aluminum aircraft are similar, but also different. These receive chromate-free exterior primers, corrosion-protective coatings, and decorative topcoats. But while the basic process is the same, they are still chromate-free. Airbus started introducing chromate-free exterior primers on the A380 in 2016, almost a decade after the Superjumbo entered service. While the Superjumbo was a clean-sheet aircraft, it was not a 'new' aircraft in the sense of the Boeing 787 and Airbus A350.

The A380 was built using the traditional aluminum-alloy fuselage. One of the criticisms of the program was that it was a big airplane more than it was a new one. Airbus and Boeing operate in different regulatory environments. The European Union has strict REACH chemical regulations that are phasing out hexavalent chromium more aggressively than in the United States.

In 2025, CIRS reported, "the European Chemicals Agency (ECHA) unveiled a proposal to restrict the use of carcinogenic hexavalent chromium (Cr(VI)) substances across the EU. The measure aims to safeguard human health and the environment from the cancer risks associated with these chemicals." This has pushed Airbus to adopt chromate-free primers faster than Boeing. These typically appear tan, beige, or pale yellow. Airbus generally doesn't use Boeing's glossy, peelable emerald green 'Spraylat' transport film. The structural primer is therefore directly visible.

Another distinctive feature of Airbus aircraft is that when assembled, their coatings are a patchwork of shades. Boeing is a national company; Airbus is a multinational company formed from the merger of major European aerospace companies. It has four home countries (the UK, France, Germany, and Spain). Accordingly, it uses a distributed European manufacturing model.

Barrels, wings, and tails are fabricated, anodized, and primed at different sites and at different subsidiaries across these countries. Each facility applies its own corrosion-inhibiting primer before they are shipped to be assembled. Those primers have slightly different chemistries, resulting in different shades. When the aircraft is pieced together, it looks like something of a patchwork of mismatched greens, yellows, and tans.

Major contractors include GKN Aerospace (Fokker Aerostructures) in the UK and the Netherlands, Latecoere in France, and Spirit AeroSystems. Airbus radomes are often matte gray or tan (made of composite materials), the nose and forward base are pale yellow-green, the center fuselage is slightly darker olive or lime green, the belly fairing is tan or beige, the wings are light gray over green primer, and the aft is olive green aluminum or tan/gray in composite areas.

The XWB-97's maturity is becoming an advantage over the rival new GE9X turbofan, helping the program to progress and be perceived as less risky.

Unlike Airbus, Boeing uses a peelable green coating to protect the aircraft's aluminum skin. The aluminum skin is relatively thin, and even minor scratches and indentations can require inspection. It protects against environmental damage during manufacturing and transport. Like Airbus, Boeing has components manufactured across a broad geological footprint, and these require transportation.

While Airbus transports many of its components on climate-controlled BelugaXL aircraft (and ferries), Boeing relies on the Open-Air Cross-Country Rail Transit for its core 737 aircraft. Those fuselages are built in Wichita, Kansas, and need to be transported 2,000 miles (3,200 km) by open railcar to Renton and Everett. Boeing's highly modified Boeing 747 Dreamlifters are used for the 787, not normally the 737. The train route is primarily operated by BNSF Railway, with the journey going through Nebraska, Wyoming, Montana, to Washington via Spokane.

The journey takes around ten to 12 days, with trains taking three to four fuselages at a time (six maximum). In 2018, Aviation Week reported, "In today’s world on the massive airplane you see going down the rail or you see entering the Renton factory the fuselage is already integrated and they are able to start the same day installing the blankets and wiring and going through the production process without having to do any structural work to the airplane."

Another key factor to consider when comparing Boeing and Airbus during their manufacturing process is that they are typically photographed at different stages of their manufacturing cycle. This creates an apples-to-oranges mismatch. Most familiar photos of green Boeing aircraft show them with the TPC peelable coating, as they are on the railcars or parked at Renton/Everett facilities.

Airbus photos are typically taken when they are being unloaded from a BelugaXL or rolling off the Final Assembly Line. They may be conducting their first customer acceptance flights. This is a later stage than the Boeing aircraft, and they typically sport a raw structural epoxy primer that gives them a flat, chalky, matte finish.

Other factors also explain why Boeing and Airbus aircraft may appear different. Boeing aircraft parked in Washington are frequently pictured in overcast skies, while Airbus aircraft in Toulouse are often in warm Mediterranean sunlight. Even the digital camera may react differently. For example, if a planespotter fills much of the frame with a green fuselage, the camera's Auto White Balance (AWB) may automatically shift the image to magenta and warm tones.

Bob's Discount Furniture: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Fuel inflation is pressing on Bob's margins, and vendor cost-sharing is the lever management is pulling to hold profit steady.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, Bob's Discount Furniture (BOBS -0.53%) announced that net revenue increased 10.3% year over year to $680 million. Store expansion and disciplined overhead management provide a path to sustained earnings growth. Three things management said on the call stand out.

Customers shopping across physical stores and digital channels spend nearly 20% more per order than average buyers. Management uses Omnicart, a digital shopping tool that lets customers start an order in store and complete it online, to capture e-commerce sales without losing physical retail touchpoints.

Management targets 10% annual store growth by opening multiple regional locations simultaneously. Launching several stores at once builds immediate brand awareness while supporting localized infrastructure, such as regional distribution centers.

Adjusted gross margin, the portion of revenue remaining after accounting for direct product and shipping expenses, dropped 30 basis points due to fuel inflation. Management relies on vendor cost-sharing and targeted price adjustments to protect profitability.

Furniture category demand shows signs of stabilizing across income cohorts, allowing the business to capture market share through low-cost operations, disciplined pricing analytics, and its omnichannel strategy, an approach that provides a seamless, consistent customer experience across every touchpoint.

Management is watching fuel surcharges and supply chain costs while preparing for upcoming Southeast expansion.

I’m Obsessed With UGG’s Ugly-Cute Ballet Sneakers (Others Disagree)

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Quill Ballet Sneakers have divided the E! shopping team, but their cozy lining and easy styling won me over. Here’s how they fit and what to know before buying.

You thought the fashion world was done with ballet sneakers? Think again! These trendy shoes just got a chilly-weather makeover. Introducing the UGG Quill Ballet Sneakers, the cozy winter slipper meets sturdy sole meets delicate pointe shoe in the brand's signature colorways and raw suede edges. In other words... some people love it, and others hate it.

This UGG ballet sneaker has truly divided the E! shopping team, with various team members swearing they're ugly and others who are absolutely obsessed with them. I'm in the latter camp. Let's face it, we don't usually go to UGG for their trendy pieces (although some other flats in their collection might be changing that), we turn to them time and time again for the cozy & casual look, and that's exactly what this shoe provides.

I got the UGG ballet sneakers in the brand's signature chestnut/sand colorway a month ago... and love them. They're so comfortable and so easy to style. I usually pair them with jeans and a casual top or sweater. They're cute in an "ugly" way. They can be worn with or without socks. I'd recommend them with socks if you're walking a lot, and I even prefer the look of them with thick socks. But wearing them with bare feet is also an easy vibe.

When it comes to sizing, I'm usually between sizes 8.5 and 9. The smaller size is for flats, heels, and everything I'd prefer to wear without socks. 9 is reserved for narrow shoes, boots, and low-profile sneakers that have a slimmer fit. For UGG's ballet sneakers, I went with an 8.5, and they're the tiniest bit too big without socks and absolutely perfect with a thick sock.

If you love the coziness of your favorite UGG slipper with the ease of styling a ballet flat and the sturdiness of a sneaker sole, I'd highly recommend the UGG Quill Ballet Sneakers—especially when certain colors are on sale!

In UGG's classic chestnut/sand colorway, these ballet sneakers feature a soft suede upper, lace-up ties that wrap around the ankle, and an ultra-plush natural sheepskin lining.

Prefer a color that goes with everything in your closet? This black colorway is the perfect neutral, with the same features as the classic shade, but with more subtle raw edges on the suede upper.

Chic, while still a colorful neutral, this high-voltage blue colorway of UGG's ballet sneaker is to die for (in this shopping writer's opinion, at least).

If you want a fiery pop of color, this (nearly sold-out) red pepper colorway of the UGG ballet sneaker is a gorgeous, cool-toned red you won't find anywhere else.

GTA 6 PS5 Codes Potentially Being Stolen from Physical Copies Using Flashlight Trick

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A potentially problematic manufacturing oversight may be resulting in some GTA 6 codes being stolen from retail PS5 cases.

Some reports claim that by pointing a strong flashlight at the side of the case, it’s possible to shine light into the empty Blu-ray box in such a way that it makes the code printed on the insert visible.

Remember, GTA 6’s physical release is a code-in-box, and doesn’t have a Blu-ray disc.

There is some debate about whether the included poster insert blocks the light and makes this impossible; some say they’ve been able to replicate the issue, while others say it can’t be done with a sealed copy.

It’s also worth noting that the US release specifically has a camouflaged pattern around the code, as shown by ResetEra user Mattmo831. That means you can certainly shine light into the box, but you can’t read the code on the other side.

Some PEGI units don’t have the camouflage pattern, so technically the code is visible using this trick, although again there’s still debate over whether the poster blocks the light in sealed copies.

It’s worth pointing out that codes aren’t activated at the point of purchase like other digital vouchers, so assuming the flashlight method works, it would be perfectly possible to pilfer a working copy of the game.

I think this story will explode if it turns out to be true, but it all just depends on how many PEGI copies don’t have the camouflage pattern and whether the inserted poster blocks the light or not.

As far as I can see, there aren’t an enormous number of people who’ve complained upon buying the physical version that their code has been redeemed – but if this method works, the publicity around it will now surely see the probability increase.

The Game Awards 2026 Nominees To Be Announced Next Week, Zelda And GTA 6 Eligible

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Geoff Keighley's annual show is almost here!

We're now at the tail end of the year, and you know what that means, right? It's time to get ready for The Game Awards.

The annual show, hosted by creator Geoff Keighley, is taking place on 10th December 2026, and will announce the nominees for each award "live" next week on 17th November 2026.

Although the highly-anticipated release Grand Theft Auto 6 isn't out until 19th November 2026, it will be eligible for nomination. This means the Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake for Switch 2, which currently has a critic score of 96/100 on Metacritic, can also be nominated.

Here's the rundown about eligibility for nomination via The Game Awards FAQ and rules pages:

Games must be made commercially available to customers on or before Friday, November 20, 2026, at 9 p.m. PT. In the Players’ Voice category, games must ship on or before November 30, 2026.

While GTA 6 and Zelda are sure to steal at least some of the spotlight, there have been plenty of other amazing games released this year. Capcom's knocked it out of the park with Resident Evil Requiem and games like Pragmata & Onimusha: Way of the Sword. And Pokémon also surprised us with the relaxing life sim Pokopia.

Once again, The Game Awards will stream live on 10th December 2026 from the Peacock Theatre in Los Angeles. As usual, you'll be able to tune in on YouTube and various other streaming platforms. For the first time, the show will also be streamed in the video game Roblox.

Kanye West Settles Lawsuit Over Sample Blocked by Descendant of Holocaust Survivors

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A federal judge agreed Thursday to dismiss a copyright infringement lawsuit against Kanye West after the musician reached a settlement with Alice Merton, the German singer-songwriter who accused West of using a sample of her song “Blindside” without permission on his track “Gun to My Head.”

Merton filed the underlying copyright infringement claim last year, saying she would never grant permission to the artist now known as Ye due to his history of “antisemitic, racist remarks.”

In her lawsuit, Merton described herself as a direct descendant of holocaust survivors who refused to clear the sample because she was “unwilling to compromise her personal beliefs.” Merton said she was “understandably shocked and humiliated” when several websites reported in December 2023 that she appeared to be a willing collaborator on Ye’s song, which featured Kid Cudi and Ty Dolla $ign.

Terms of the new settlement were not disclosed in the order dismissing the action signed by U.S. District Court Judge Fernando L. Aenlle-Rocha and obtained by Rolling Stone. In a Nov. 6 court filing, lawyers for both sides said they had agreed to resolve the dispute and expected to seek dismissal of the lawsuit once the settlement terms had been fulfilled. Attempts to reach the lawyers were not immediately successful Friday.

Ye first teased “Gun to My Head” at an early Vultures listening event in December 2023. The song garnered particular attention because it marked Ye and Cudi’s first collaboration in several years.

According to the lawsuit, “Gun to My Head” sampled a segment of “Blindside” that “spans most of the entire song’s baseline melody.” It also featured a vocal recording of the opening lyric line from Merton’s song, “I sat down with a gun to my head,” which became “the dominant lyric line” in Ye’s song, the complaint said.

Merton said Ye had not contacted her to clear the sample before the listening event, and the song did not appear on the first Vultures album released Feb. 8, 2024. But on Feb. 15, 2024, Ye’s clearance company allegedly reached out to clear the sample, and a few weeks later, Merton denied the request, saying Ye’s “values are contrary to our values,” the lawsuit said. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

When Vultures 2 arrived in August 2024, “Gun to My Head” was not included on the official track list, but it was briefly released on a digital deluxe version of the album. Ye also played it at other listening parties, and it ended up in bootlegged versions available on YouTube, the lawsuit said.

When the song did not make the Vultures 2 cut, Ye’s fans allegedly “blamed Merton for not authorizing” the sample and even sent her “death threats,” he complaint alleged. She said Ye “did nothing to stop the abuse, allowing his fans to intimidate and harass [her].”

The lawsuit was not the first to accuse Ye of using copyrighted music without permission at a listening event. In May, a jury found that he had infringed on the copyright of an unreleased demo by sampling it in an early version of his Grammy-winning song “Hurricane,” which he played for 40,000 fans at a sold-out Donda listening event in 2021.

During the six-day trial, lawyers for the plaintiffs — four musicians who composed the one-minute instrumental “MSD PT2” — argued that their clients were entitled to a share of the estimated $5.6 million Ye earned from ticket and merchandise sales at the July 2021 event at Mercedes-Benz Stadium in Atlanta, as well as a deal with Apple Music to stream the show. Trending Stories Pink Floyd’s Roger Waters Files for Divorce From Fourth Wife Kamilah Chavis After 5 Years of Marriage The ‘You Can See Everything’ Interview You’ve Been Waiting For John Mulaney Has Many Thoughts on the 2026 Rock Hall Inductees — And Picks Next Year’s Class Dolly Parton Heals the Soul on Final Recording, ‘Torn,’ a Duet With Bono on U2’s New Album

The jury found Ye personally liable for $176,153 and his company Yeezy LLC liable for an additional $176,153. Two other companies, Yeezy Supply and Mascotte Holdings, were found liable for $41,625 and $44,627, respectively, bringing the total award to $438,558.

A spokesman for Ye called the verdict a “failed shakedown,” saying the plaintiffs had demanded $30 million to settle before trial.

SpaceX launches Starship in key test flight of massive rocket

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In a key test flight on Friday, SpaceX tried but failed to recover both stages of its massive Starship rocket.

The rocket lifted off from the company's South Texas launch facility at 4:47 p.m. local time, or 5:47 p.m. ET. SpaceX had to abort its plans to catch and reuse the booster, instead diverting it to splash into the Gulf of Mexico, where it burst into flames.

Test Flight 15, the most ambitious Starship mission to date, was the first time Elon Musk's company attempted to catch both the booster and upper stage, or spacecraft portion, of the massive rocket.

Earlier on Friday, SpaceX obtained authorization from the Federal Aviation Administration. Hours later, the FAA issued a nationwide ground stop after reporting communication problems affecting "multiple" air traffic control centers. The SpaceX license wasn't effected by the flight stoppage, a spokesperson for the FAA told CNBC.

The upper stage of the Starship continued its trajectory into orbit after the company failed to catch and salvage the booster. The rocket is expected to fly around the Earth fourteen times in total. If successful, SpaceX will land the upper stage back at its Starbase facility on Saturday.

Starship's success is critical to SpaceX and its ambitions to eventually lift cargo and people to the surface of the Moon and Mars. In the near-term, SpaceX needs to send more satellites into orbit to expands its Starlink satellite internet service, which is currently the company's profit center.

The company's constellation already includes over 11,000 active satellites in low Earth orbit. One Starship rocket has the capacity to carry up to 60 Starlink v3 satellites into orbit, the company has said, representing about 20 times the capacity of earlier Falcon 9 rockets.

In the previous Starship test flight, the company released 26 v3 satellites, and released another 26 in the Friday test flight, with the goal of "Starlink becoming 51% of the internet," SpaceX said in a post on X.

When it comes to launches, "SpaceX is likely a full decade ahead of peers on this front," William Blair analysts wrote in a note out on Friday. "Even if tonight's catch is unsuccessful, a delay by a few months is not that consequential."

Meanwhile, SpaceX is facing fresh criticism over restrictions imposed on its Starshield satellite internet service in Ukraine. Starshield is built on Starlink technology and is the military-tier of SpaceX's satellite internet service.

Earlier this week, Ukraine President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said his country's military forces had experienced limits on Starshield without warning again, harming their ability to defend against Russia's ongoing aerial attacks. Similar limits on Starshield in Ukraine were temporarily imposed last month.

Some U.S. senators have condemned the restrictions, and urged Congress to investigate SpaceX.

How Agent Anomaly Detection scores on benchmarks we didn't write

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AI agents now read email, book travel and move money for the people who use them. Every tool call also pulls in text that someone else wrote, and whoever controls that text can try to steer the agent. OWASP calls this agent goal hijack and ranks it first in its Top 10 for Agentic Applications (2026).

In September we announced Agent Anomaly Detection in Private Preview on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform. It reads your agents' traces and logs asynchronously, without adding latency, and flags sessions where an agent acts outside its intended boundaries. We tested this feature against benchmarks for this top risk of agent goal hijack.

Security tools tend to look good on tests their own team wrote, so we ran five non-Google-written, public agent-security benchmarks through Agent Anomaly Detection without changing anything. In four of them, the harmful instruction hides in data that a tool returns. In the fifth, the user asks for harm directly.

The model itself didn't follow about half of the harmful instructions it read. It refused three in four direct requests for harm, and ignored or failed at about 30% of the instructions hidden in tool output. Of the ones that were executed, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% for post-execution review. It raised false alarms on 2% to 16% of benign sessions, depending on the tools the agent uses. Poisoned memory is the clear gap: when instructions planted in the agent’s long-term memory made it act, Agent Anomaly Detection caught 4 of 18.

We ran each benchmark case through an agent built with the Agent Development Kit (ADK) using gemini-2.5-flash, on the benchmark's simulated apps. The agent sent its traces and logs to Agent Anomaly Detection, as a customer's agent would. Then the benchmark's own Python check read the final state of the apps, such as whether money left the account, and labeled the session. Deterministic code decides the label, not a model, so an injected instruction can't mislead the grader, and Agent Anomaly Detection cannot cheat the benchmark. Each agent first ran at least 300 benign sessions, because Agent Anomaly Detection learns how each agent usually behaves.

We also recorded whether a tool returned the harmful instruction to the agent. In many cases, the agent never opens the email or file that holds it. So each harmful session ends one of three ways:

In AgentHarm, the user sends the request, so the agent always reads it.

The five benchmarks are AgentDojo (NeurIPS 2024), InjecAgent (ACL 2024 Findings), Agent Security Bench (ASB, ICLR 2025), AgentDyn (2026) and AgentHarm (ICLR 2025). ASB plants the instruction in the agent's long-term memory. In all, Agent Anomaly Detection scored 1,774 sessions.

Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% of the harmful instructions that the agent carried out, and raised a few false alarms on ordinary office work. It's strongest on goal hijack and tool misuse, and weakest on poisoned memory.

Each finding carries a threat category, and four of Agent Anomaly Detection’s five categories align with the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications. We graded each OWASP category on what this study showed, not on what Agent Anomaly Detection is designed to catch.

Public benchmarks concentrate on goal hijack and tool misuse, so our evidence is strongest there. We did not find public benchmarks with labeled agent traces for cascading failures or for identity and privilege abuse. Shared benchmarks for those categories would help the whole field.

By benchmark, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 85% of the carried-out harmful instructions on AgentDojo, 87% on InjecAgent, 99% on AgentDyn and 81% on AgentHarm. Over all harmful sessions, including the ones that the agent never read or resisted, it flagged 45% to 62%. In the 141 sessions where the agent never read the instruction, nothing happened, and it flagged just 1.

Results held across the apps. In 8 of the 11 apps, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged at least 95% of the carried-out harmful instructions. The lowest rate was in AgentDojo's Slack app, at 63%. Many of those instructions ask for one small action, such as a page visit, which looks like normal browsing.

Agent Anomaly Detection also flags many requests that the agent refuses. In AgentHarm, the agent refused most requests and carried out 108 of 412, and Agent Anomaly Detection still flagged 55% of the refused sessions, because the request sits in the user's own message, which it reads. When the agent didn't follow an instruction hidden in tool output, it flagged only 11% to 25%, because the instruction sits deep inside a tool result.

False alarms were rare on ordinary office work: on the three injection benchmarks, whose benign sessions are office tasks, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 2% to 4% of benign sessions. On AgentHarm it flagged 16%. Those benign tasks are twins of the harmful ones, so they post to social media, run terminal commands and download files. High-impact tools in normal use look more like misuse.

Agent Anomaly Detection did less well in five areas. It flagged only 11% to 25% of the instructions that the agent resisted, because a resisted instruction changes little in how the agent behaves. It missed more small actions, like single page visits, because they look like normal work. And it raised more false alarms for agents whose everyday work uses high-impact tools. If your agent is one of them, expect more findings at first, and triage them by severity and threat category.

The other two areas are new agents and poisoned memory. Some Agent Anomaly Detection methods need a few hundred sessions of history: on AgentDyn, it flagged 33% of carried-out harmful instructions when each agent had about 220 benign sessions, and 99% with 360. And it caught only 4 of the 18 sessions where poisoned memory made the agent act.

Protect your agents with defense in depth. Use controls that stop harmful actions before they run, and Agent Anomaly Detection to find what gets through.

We used one agent model and simulated apps, and the benchmarks' benign tasks are likely simpler than your production traffic, so your false-alarm rate can differ. Agent Anomaly Detection returned no result for about 6% of the sessions we sent; counting those as misses lowers the overall rates by 1 to 2 points.

Agent Anomaly Detection is in Private Preview. To request access, read the Agent Anomaly Detection overview.
