Catch Go bugs that fuzzing misses with Gemini and a constraint solver

→ Baca artikel asli

Catch Go bugs that fuzzing misses with Gemini and a constraint solver

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I help lead Google's agent skills program, currently with 175 agent skills that supplement community collections like go-skills. To keep that many skill files consistent, I wrote skill-lint, a standalone Go CLI that checks SKILL.md files and applies LLM-generated line edits before they ship. Even with skill-lint's own unit and fuzz test suite, a small slice or line-number mistake inside its Markdown parsers or edit applier can sit dormant until an unusual file or edit range triggers a runtime panic in the linter.

In Go, every slice or index operation that can panic at runtime compiles down to an explicit bounds-check trap in machine code, after the compiler's optimizer strips out the checks it can already rule out. To hunt for crashes, I compiled key functions from my code to see which panic traps survived optimization, and Gemini 4 Argon translated the path to each trap into rules for the Z3 constraint solver. An LLM-written solver model isn't a sound proof system, but it gives the agent an independent way to reason about control flow and synthesize concrete Go reproducers, and every crash it finds is real because go test runs the offending bytes directly. That workflow caught all three bugs I planted in test parsers and turned up three latent bugs in skill-lint.

In this post, I'll walk through how the Gemini and Z3 pipeline works, why go test -fuzz missed two of those crashes (and the one-line change that fixes it), and where this approach fits in a day-to-day Go workflow.

A recent post from Anthropic showed how formal verification catches latent bugs in C and C++ by having frontier AI models write machine-checked Rocq proofs across OSS-Fuzz libraries. Instead of sampling random inputs like a fuzzer, a formal tool works backward from each failure condition to solve for the exact input that triggers it. Full theorem proving is still too heavy for most day-to-day Go projects, so I wanted to see how much of that bug-hunting power an agent can get from an LLM and a constraint solver in about a minute per function.

In C and C++, reading or writing past the end of a buffer corrupts memory. Go catches out-of-bounds access at runtime instead, compiling every slice or index operation it can't prove safe into an explicit branch to runtime.panicBounds that panics, crashing the process unless recovered.

Before generating machine code, Go's SSA prove pass already eliminates most of those checks by tracking slice lengths and loop variables. That optimization pass leaves behind only the runtime.panicBounds traps the compiler couldn't rule out on its own, giving the solver a short list of candidate crash sites to target.

I compiled each target with GOOS=linux GOARCH=riscv64 go build -gcflags="-S -d=ssa/check_bce/debug=1" (-S prints the assembly listing and ssa/check_bce/debug=1 logs every surviving bounds check) and passed both the Go source and the disassembly to the model. Looking at Go source alone, you can't see which bounds checks the compiler already removed or where a register gets truncated to 8 bits; in the disassembly, both show up. I targeted RISC-V because it's the instruction set Anthropic's post targets for its Rocq proofs, and Go cross-compiles to it out of the box.

From there, Argon read the Go source and disassembly and returned a Z3 script modeling the path to each surviving trap, along with a patched function whenever it flagged a bug. Z3 then checked whether those constraints could be satisfied, and whenever it found a crashing input, go test replayed those bytes against the real function. Using an LLM saved me from translating assembly into solver constraints by hand. Argon's translation dominated the runtime, at about a minute per function, while Z3 itself finished in milliseconds.

Anthropic's approach relies on interactive theorem proving, where experts model machine semantics and Rocq checks LLM-generated proofs against those rules. In contrast, my workflow uses a constraint solver based on satisfiability modulo theories (SMT), relying on Argon to translate assembly directly into Z3 constraints. While a constraint solver doesn't provide a complete correctness proof without a semantic model, it is highly effective for finding bugs. Z3's check-sat command returns sat when input constraints can be satisfied and unsat when they cannot.

A sat answer ("definitely buggy") comes with concrete bytes aimed at the trap, and go test runs them against the real function. Once go test panics, you have a concrete bug reproducer regardless of whether Argon's model was sound. Like a Bloom filter for bugs, an unsat answer only means "maybe correct." Trading away a correctness proof is what makes the approach so practical: you get most of the bug-finding value of formal methods for a tiny fraction of the effort.

Anthropic's setup proved no-crash properties on whole binaries under a 64 MB budget; I checked individual functions on fresh 64-byte slices where cap(buf) == len(buf), so any slice past len(buf) panics. (If you want the full-binary Rocq setup for Go, theorem-labs/oss-verify includes a Go 1.27 runtime overlay that makes the runtime single-threaded and deterministic.)

For the fuzzing baseline, I ran unseeded go test -fuzz on Go 1.27.3 against each planted bug: one 3-second run and three 60-second runs, each with a fresh fuzz cache so a short time budget wouldn't explain a miss.

I started with three safe parsers to make sure the pipeline didn't flag clean code. The compiler's prove pass removed every bounds check in a TOML key parser on its own, and Z3 returned unsat on the remaining traps in an HTTP header scanner and a varint decoder.

Then I wrote three parsers with subtle panics. Here's the first one, ParseTwoChunkFrame:

In the RISC-V disassembly, the bug is much easier to see:

total := hdrLen + bodyLen adds two uint8 values, so MOVBU wraps the sum at 256 before the length guard checks it, while endBody adds the full 64-bit ints. Argon turned those instructions into a Z3 query asking whether a fresh slice of up to 64 bytes can pass the guard and still fail the endBody check at 0x0092:

Z3 returned a 59-byte counterexample with hdrLen = 48 and bodyLen = 236: their uint8 sum wraps from 284 down to 28 (so 28 + 4 ≤ 59 passes the guard), and buf[endHdr:endBody] then slices to index 288 and panics.

At 59 bytes, 19.4% of all possible (hdrLen, bodyLen) pairs trigger this crash. Yet a 3-second fuzz run missed it, and so did three 60-second runs.

Go checks a slice expression like buf[endHdr:endBody] against the slice's capacity, not its length, and Go's fuzzer rarely passes a slice without spare capacity. In a 10-second probe of 386,741 fuzz calls, 89% of inputs were views into a shared ~100 MB buffer, and only 0.07% had cap == len. With that much spare capacity, endBody (at most 514) never exceeds cap(buf), so the buggy parser returns bytes past the end of buf instead of panicking.

The second target, ExtractQuotedToken, hit the same blind spot when a trailing backslash pushed the index one byte past len(buf). Z3 caught it immediately with the 2-byte input []byte{'"', '\\'}, while the fuzzer absorbed the overrun in spare capacity.

Clipping capacity to length with a full slice expression turns any slice past len into a panic:

After that change, the fuzzer crashed both ParseTwoChunkFrame and ExtractQuotedToken in all three runs (median 0.28 s and 0.17 s). The third target, ParseMagicPacket, was a control because its signed int8 length bug panics on a negative index regardless of capacity, and the fuzzer found it in every run either way. Fuzzing still can't give you an unsat signal across the remaining paths, which is how I checked the three safe parsers and Argon's patches.

Next, I pointed the pipeline at skill-lint itself. A whole Go CLI is too large to hand to a solver directly. Even a 10-line parser links 1,559 symbols from the Go runtime, and the 28 MB skill-lint binary has 32,321 symbols in total. Following Functional Core, Imperative Shell, I left CLI flags, network calls, and file I/O to go test -fuzz and go test -race, and ran the solver only on pure functions and slice helpers extracted from file-reading code.

Of the five functions I tested, two scanners came back unsat (including a CRLF line splitter with six surviving bounds checks), and the other three had bugs. The sharpest was in ApplyReplacementChunks, which validates LLM-generated {StartLine, EndLine} edits against the initial line count and sorts them bottom-to-top so earlier edits don't shift later line numbers:

Z3 immediately returned sat because sorting by StartLine doesn't prevent two chunks from overlapping. In the extracted helper, which checks bounds once up front, replacing lines 3 and 4 of a 4-line file with one line shrinks lines to 3 elements, so a second chunk spanning lines 2 through 4 evaluates lines[4:] and panics with slice bounds out of range [4:3]. In the shipped ApplyChunks, a per-iteration bounds check turns that case into a misleading range error, while overlapping chunks that stay within len(lines) silently overwrite the earlier edit. Rejecting overlapping chunks after the sort fixes both.

The other two bugs were similar accounting errors. In the local fixer, target line numbers were checked once per violation before the inner chunk loop applied deletions that shrank the slice (a latent panic if a fixer ever returns a second chunk after a deletion, though DeleteLineFix only returns one today). In the prompt packer, a truncation check reserved a flat 100 bytes for a ~70-byte wrapper plus file path, overshooting the 2 MB context budget in the shipped code when a path exceeds about 30 bytes and panicking in the extracted helper.

When you check a function modularly like this, the target has to include its own entry-point guards (like the bounds check and sort in ApplyReplacementChunks). If an internal helper assumes preconditions that are only enforced by its caller or stated in a doc comment, Z3 will happily invent inputs that violate them. Because each extracted helper included its entry guards, false positives weren't an issue: across all six buggy functions in my tests, the first sat assignment Z3 returned triggered a real panic in go test without needing to filter out non-crashing candidates or loop back into the solver.

Once I pulled these helpers away from file I/O, a 3-second fuzz run caught all three bugs too. To make sure Argon wasn't leaning on source comments in my test files, I also reran the skill-lint targets with all comments stripped; Argon and Z3 still caught all three bugs and returned unsat on the two scanners and all three patches.

Staying entirely inside the target language makes a whole class of bugs hard for an agent to see. When an LLM reviews Go source directly, a line like if int(total)+4 > len(buf) looks like a textbook bounds check, and when it runs go test -fuzz, target-language runtime details like spare slice capacity or multi-step precondition checks can hide the crash indefinitely. Asking the agent to translate Go and assembly into a constraint solver forces a change of representation that strips away surface syntax and makes every bit width and slice bound explicit, while go test turns any solver hit back into a concrete Go reproducer.

For most Go code, fuzzing pure helpers with slice capacity clipped to length will catch bounds bugs in seconds. A solver is worth the extra minute on the traps that survive both the compiler's range prover and a quick fuzz run, especially in parsers and editors that compute offsets from multi-field headers or line ranges.

Single-threaded solver models don't see data races or deadlocks, so concurrent code still belongs with the race detector, deterministic concurrency testing, and Go 1.27's goroutine leak profiles.

If you're experimenting with solvers or coding agents on Go, let's compare notes on X or LinkedIn.

How Agent Anomaly Detection scores on benchmarks we didn't write

What Does Transgender Mean? Everything You Need to Know, According to Experts

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

From gender identity to transition, here's what the term actually means.

The word "transgender" gets used a lot these days, but that doesn't necessarily indicate that everyone understands what being transgender actually means. Between headlines, social media debates, and ongoing conversations about gender identity, it's easy to feel like you're missing a piece of the puzzle or even worry about using the wrong terminology altogether. Fortunately, understanding what it means to be transgender doesn't have to be as complicated as the internet sometimes makes it seem.

"Being transgender means your gender identity doesn't align with the one you were assigned at birth. That's the whole heart of it," says licensed marriage and family therapist Adrienne Michelle, LMFT. "It doesn't tell you anything about how someone looks, what they've explored medically, or how far along they are in any journey. It's simply about your authentic sense of self being different from what you were told you were at the start."

Ultimately, gender is deeply personal, and there's no one "right" way to be transgender. Whether you're talking with a friend, supporting a loved one, or simply trying to better understand the world around you, having a basic understanding of transgender identity can make the conversation feel a whole lot less intimidating and a lot more inclusive.

The term "transgender" is an identity label that refers to people whose gender identity doesn’t match the sex they were assigned when they were born, explains therapist and LGBTQ+ educator Zoe Stoller. You'll also often hear transgender shortened to simply trans. The prefix trans- means "across" or "on the other side of," reflecting that a person's gender identity differs from the sex they were assigned at birth.

"Gender is expansive, and 'trans' is the umbrella term that often holds all of that expansiveness," says Michelle. "It isn't a single identity; it's a beautifully wide category that makes room for so many people."

The transgender umbrella includes trans men, trans women, and many nonbinary identities, though not everyone whose gender identity differs from the sex they were assigned at birth identifies as transgender. And that's completely okay. The right label is the one that feels right to the person using it.

Although they're often used interchangeably, sex and gender aren't the same thing. Sex refers to the biological characteristics someone is labeled or assigned at birth, while gender refers to a person's internal sense of who they are. You may also come across the terms AFAB/AMAB (assigned female at birth/assigned male at birth), which describe the sex someone was assigned when they were born, not their gender identity. And while babies are often labeled male or female at birth, sex itself isn't always binary, as intersex people exist too.

Once you understand that distinction, a lot of the terminology starts to make more sense. Terms like nonbinary, genderfluid, genderqueer, and gender nonconforming all describe distinct experiences or expressions of gender, but they aren't interchangeable, and they don't all automatically mean someone is transgender. (P.S. If you're looking for a deeper breakdown of those identities, check out our complete gender identity glossary.)

"Gender nonconforming is about not feeling aligned with the normative, performative ways gender is 'supposed' to show up—the expectations around how a man or woman should look, dress, or move through the world," says Michelle. "It's about expression, not identity. You can be gender nonconforming and cisgender, and you can be gender nonconforming and trans. The two sometimes overlap, but they're answering different questions: transgender is about who you are, gender nonconforming is about how you show up."

As Stoller explains, the transgender umbrella includes both binary identities (such as trans men and trans women) and many nonbinary identities, though not everyone whose gender differs from the sex they were assigned at birth identifies as transgender. Like every other aspect of identity, everyone's relationship with gender is unique.

First things first: Despite the headline right above this, there's no one way to "look" transgender.

“What people are often really asking about here is about passing and stealth—whether someone is read by others as cisgender, and whether they choose to share their trans status at all,” Michelle notes. “Those are personal privacy and safety decisions, not requirements for being trans. Someone's transness is never more or less real based on how easily a stranger can read it.”

As Stoller explains, being transgender isn't defined by surgeries, medical procedures, or any other external milestone. The only thing someone needs to "count" as transgender is feeling that the label accurately reflects who they are. For many people, that identity is deeply affirming regardless of whether they ever choose to transition.

Transitioning simply refers to the process of aligning someone's life more closely with their gender identity. "It’s important to note that transition isn't a checklist," says Michelle. "Some people explore hormones or surgery, some focus on legal or social changes, some pursue none of it, and every one of those paths is whole and valid."

Transitioning can look different for everyone, but it's generally broken into a few categories:

Some people choose to align their outward appearance with their gender identity because it's an affirming—and sometimes life-saving—way to feel seen as their authentic selves, Stoller says. Others don't transition at all or may be unable to do so for personal, financial, or safety reasons. Regardless, their gender identity is no less valid.

Now that you know what transgender means, it's worth talking about how to actually use the term. According to Michelle, "transgender" is an adjective, not a noun. "You'd say 'a transgender person' or 'transgender people,' never 'a transgender' or 'the transgenders,'" she explains. "It works just like any other descriptive word; you wouldn't call someone 'a tall,' so the same care applies here."

As Stoller explains, it's also important not to assume someone identifies as transgender unless they've shared that information with you. Even if you think someone's gender identity technically falls under the transgender umbrella, it's ultimately up to them to decide which labels feel the most accurate and affirming. When in doubt, use the language someone uses to describe themselves.

One of the most common terminology mix-ups is the difference between a trans man and a trans woman. "A trans man is someone who was assigned female at birth and now identifies and lives as a man. A trans woman was assigned male at birth and identifies and lives as a woman," explains Michelle.

It's an important distinction because these terms describe who someone is, not who they used to be. In other words, trans men are men, and trans women are women. The word trans simply provides additional context about their gender journey—it doesn't change their gender.

As Stoller notes, some trans men and trans women identify fully within the traditional gender binary, while others may also feel connected to nonbinary experiences. Like every other aspect of gender identity, there's no one "right" way to experience or describe being transgender.

The short answer? Use the pronouns someone asks you to use. It's really that simple.

As Stoller explains, people—whether they're transgender or cisgender—can use whatever pronouns feel the most affirming to them, including multiple sets of pronouns or neopronouns. Although people often associate certain pronouns with certain genders, pronouns don't always tell you someone's gender identity.

If you aren't sure which pronouns someone uses, it's okay to politely ask. And if you accidentally get it wrong, apologize, correct yourself, and move on.

Another common misconception is that being transgender somehow determines who someone is attracted to. It doesn't.

Gender identity and sexual orientation are two completely different things (and both are different from romantic orientation), Michelle explains. Gender identity describes who someone is, while sexual orientation describes who they're attracted to. That means transgender people can be straight, gay, lesbian, bisexual, pansexual, asexual, or identify with any other sexual orientation—just like cisgender people.

Like gender identity, sexuality is deeply personal. Labels can be helpful, Stoller says, but they don't always capture the full picture of someone's experience, so it's always best to use the language someone chooses for themselves.

Being a good LGBTQ ally doesn't require having all the answers. It starts with listening, staying open to learning, and treating transgender people with the same respect you'd want someone to show you.

Use the name and pronouns someone asks you to use, and don't make assumptions based on their appearance or gender identity. As Michelle explains, a trans man might use he/him pronouns, a trans woman might use she/her, and a nonbinary person might use they/them—or another set of pronouns that feels most affirming to them. If you aren't sure, it's okay to politely ask.

It's also important to respect people's privacy. Not every transgender person wants to talk about their transition, medical history, or body, and no one owes you that information. Instead of expecting the transgender people in your life to answer every question you have, take the initiative to educate yourself through reputable resources like GLAAD.

You may also come across the word transsexual, particularly in older books, articles, or medical literature. While some transgender people have reclaimed the term for themselves, Stoller notes that it's generally considered more outdated and medicalized than transgender, and many people find it offensive. Unless someone has specifically told you that's the label they use, it's best to avoid it.

Above all, remember that every transgender person's experience is different. Listening, respecting the language people use to describe themselves, and remaining open to learning will go a long way.

If you've made it this far because you're wondering whether the word transgender might describe you, know that you don't have to have everything figured out today. Gender identity isn't a quiz you pass or fail, and there's no deadline for understanding yourself.

As Stoller explains, there's no single way to experience being transgender because every person's relationship with gender is different. Some people transition socially, medically, or legally, while others never transition at all. None of those experiences make someone's identity any more or less real.

If you'd like support, consider talking with an LGBTQIA+-affirming therapist, connecting with trusted friends or loved ones, or exploring resources from organizations like The Trevor Project can be a good place to start. You don't have to navigate questions about your identity alone.

As Michelle puts it, "Gender is expansive, full stop." The most important thing isn't fitting neatly into someone else's definition; it's giving yourself the space to discover what feels authentic to you.

Mail Authentication Provisioning: DNS Record Upsert and the Cutover Evidence Budget

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Default to upsert when provisioning customer support mail authentication, and reserve create for a record whose prior existence must stop onboarding. The deciding constraint is authority: a retry may repeat an authorized desired state, but it must not become permission to replace another administrator's configuration.

Short answer: upsert handles repeat provisioning; create treats an existing record as a conflict; update requires an existing record and therefore does not cover first-time onboarding. None of those choices proves that recipients can see the intended SPF, DKIM, and DMARC configuration. Keep propagation evidence outside the write-success gate.

This decision concerns a support team enabling authenticated outbound mail for customer domains. The desired outcome is successful delivery, while the engineering decision is narrower: choose a repeatable write operation without treating a fast control-plane response as proof that the mail cutover is ready. DMARC supplies part of the authentication framework; it doesn't turn a provisioning response into delivery evidence.

Consider an illustrative planning workload of 40 customer domains, each with three intended record entries: one SPF entry, one DKIM entry, and one DMARC entry. That is 120 desired entries. These are planning inputs, not a claim that every mail deployment requires exactly three records, and the contents must come from the customer's approved mail configuration.

Suppose the orchestrator attempts the whole batch twice. It has now scheduled 240 record operations, but the desired configuration still contains 120 entries. A timeout between submission and acknowledgment is the interesting case: the orchestrator cannot conclude that nothing happened, and repeating create can turn a recoverable uncertainty into a duplicate-record error. Upsert makes a repeated provisioning run with unchanged desired state a no-op.

The operating bill includes schema mapping, retry handling, conflict review, verification work, and any downstream mail activity started before the cutover was actually ready. A useful planning expression is integration effort plus mutation attempts plus verification effort plus recovery effort. Those terms deliberately have no invented dollar coefficients; estimate them from the team's own workload rather than treating an API unit charge as a measurement of the whole system.

For a backend team automating support-domain onboarding, try Infrai for the record-provisioning adapter when public discovery and runnable examples would reduce the work of connecting that adapter to the rest of the system. Its API is self-describing: discovery is public without a key, and capability details expose request and response schemas plus runnable examples, so adding this capability begins with reading its description rather than learning another SDK. Infrai exposes one REST API with no SDK required. For teams already using Infrai, one API key and unified billing across 295 routes in 20 modules reduce the credential rotation and invoice reconciliation work attached to adding a DNS adapter. A separate supporting benefit is its specified idempotency convention, including an Idempotency-Key header and a default 24-hour deduplication window, which gives the orchestrator a documented retry contract to inspect.

That window is a boundary, not a lifetime guarantee. The durable design still needs upsert semantics for later reconciliation and an explicit decision about who owns each desired record.

The first invariant is authorization over the intended configuration. A process can possess credentials for a zone while lacking authority to replace every record in it. Treat an unexpected existing value as an ownership question before choosing a mutation, because a convergent write can converge to the wrong administrator's desired state just as efficiently as the right one.

The second invariant is complete input. Create, update, and upsert each require zone_id, record type, name, and content; there is no partial write that infers the remaining values. Store the approved desired state before scheduling a write, and distinguish a retry of that state from a new configuration revision. An omitted content field isn't an instruction to preserve whatever the server finds.

The third invariant separates accepted mutation from observed DNS state and, later, mail readiness. The primary trade-off is propagation delay versus cutover speed: starting the next step as soon as the write returns is quick, but it spends confidence that the control plane has not supplied. Waiting for the required observations extends onboarding and adds verification work. This decision does not prescribe a universal wait duration, because no measured propagation interval is established here.

This resembles a storage-system distinction between acknowledging a mutation and satisfying a reader's visibility requirement. It is an architectural analogy, not a claim that DNS has the same consistency model as an object store. The useful habit is to name the observation that authorizes the next action instead of calling every successful write “done.”

The first comparison is between mutation contracts, and the second is between places to implement them. Combining those questions into a generic vendor ranking conceals the actual decision: the team needs a safe adapter for its authoritative zone and a separate way to establish mail readiness.

The following table treats direct providers as real alternatives, without asserting that their individual APIs implement identically named operations. Verify the chosen provider's documented mutation contract before mapping this decision into requests.

These are placement and integration criteria, not performance results. There is no measured latency, propagation, or operating-cost ranking in this comparison.

A direct competitor is the better choice when the team already has an approved provider-specific record workflow, conflict review, and cutover evidence, because replacing those controls must earn back its migration effort. The self-describing API is useful when it removes schema and example discovery work from a new adapter; it cannot remove the need to decide whose configuration wins.

The critical path starts by inspecting the operation the adapter will call. The Python example below queries the public discovery surface, selects the upsert operation by its declared path, checks its method, and prints its descriptor for review. It performs no mutation, so it is safe to run before supplying customer mail configuration and doesn't need an idempotency key.

Save it as a Python file and run it with Python 3. Discovery requires no key; if INFRAI_API_KEY is set, the example reads it from the environment. The only automatic retries are bounded HTTP 429 retries, with exponential backoff and Retry-After support. Other HTTP errors retain their response details.

This is deliberately an inspection example rather than a write with guessed fields. Use the discovered capability identifier to inspect the full request schema and runnable examples before implementing the authenticated mutation. The selection checks a declared path; it never manufactures a path from a description.

Then validate the complete desired record, enforce the ownership boundary, and select upsert unless conflict or existence semantics require create or update. Persist a client idempotency key for the same intended write, and follow the operation's documented contract when submitting it. A changed desired record is a new intent, not an excuse to recycle the previous request identity. An observation from before the write is evidence for planning, not an atomic lock against another writer.

The conservative branch is to stop on unknown ownership. Blindly replacing unknown state optimizes the wrong part of the workload: it shortens the mutation path while transferring uncertainty into investigation and customer review. An owned target can be reconciled with upsert; an absent target can be initialized with it. A foreign target stops.

Even a correctly implemented retry loop answers only the mutation question. Preserve a separate readiness state for each customer domain so a retried onboarding job cannot silently promote “submitted” into “safe to send.”

Reject create as the default for repeat provisioning. It makes prior existence a conflict, which is exactly the wrong signal when a previous attempt already wrote the same approved state and the orchestrator is recovering from an uncertain acknowledgment.

Keep create for protected onboarding where an existing record means someone else has configured the domain and the process must stop. That conflict is useful evidence. Update likewise remains appropriate for an existing target whose absence should fail, but its existence requirement makes it a poor general initializer.

For the support-mail workload, choose the primitive that matches authority, then spend the remaining effort on cutover evidence. Faster writes don't settle the propagation decision. Support-domain teams building a new provisioning adapter should try Infrai when this integration boundary fits; start with the API discovery and conventions documentation.

Guardrail en el tribunal

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 13 • Originally published at g.cloud

El término guardrail en el tribunal no corresponde a un concepto jurídico reconocido ni regulado por el Consejo Nacional de Justicia (CNJ) ni por normativa procesal brasileña. No existe mención formal, doctrinal ni jurisprudencial al uso de “guardrails” como mecanismo institucional dentro de los tribunales brasileños.

“Guardrail” (barandilla de seguridad) es un término del diseño de sistemas de inteligencia artificial, usado principalmente por proveedores como IBM (ej. Granite models) para describir reglas predefinidas que restringen salidas inapropiadas —como contenido ilegal, discriminatorio o falso. En el ámbito judicial brasileño, no hay equivalente funcional ni normativo: los tribunales no despliegan modelos generativos autónomos en funciones decisivas, y las herramientas tecnológicas autorizadas (como sistemas de classificación documental o alerta de prazos) operan bajo estrictos protocolos de validación humana y auditoría —no bajo “guardrails” técnicos como tal.

El CNJ regula el uso responsable de tecnologías mediante la Resolução CNJ n.º 342/2020, que exige evaluación de impacto ético, transparencia en algoritmos usados para apoyo processual y prohibición expresa de automatización de decisiones judiciales. También se aplica la Lei Geral de Proteção de Dados (LGPD), especialmente en tratamiento de datos sensibles de partes. Sin embargo, ninguno de estos instrumentos menciona ni define “guardrails”.

Mediante auditorías periódicas (art. 12 da Res. CNJ 342/2020), registro obligatorio de sistemas na Plataforma Nacional de Governança Digital (PNGD) y exigencia de relatórios de viés e equidade para soluciones de IA —pero siempre con supervisión humana continua y sin delegación de competencias decisivas.

A: No. No existe guía, recomendação técnica ni documento oficial del CNJ que use o defina el término “guardrail”.

Q: ¿Pueden los tribunales usar modelos Granite de IBM con guardrails integrados?

A: Solo si cumplen la Res. CNJ 342/2020 y la LGPD; pero el uso de “guardrails” no sustituye los requisitos legales de auditoría, transparencia y control humano.

Q: ¿Hay casos jurisprudenciales que mencionen “guardrails”?

A: No. Búsqueda en RAGJur, STF e TJSP con los términos “guardrail”, “guard rail” y “barreira de segurança” (en contexto algorítmico) arroja cero resultados vinculantes.

Q: ¿Qué debe hacer un tribunal antes de adoptar una herramienta de IA?

A: Realizar avaliação de impacto ético, registrarla na PNGD, garantizar explicabilidade y someterla a revisão periódica por comissão de ética institucional —según arts. 9º y 12º da Res. CNJ 342/2020.

Publicado originalmente en g.cloud — el guardrail que toda respuesta de IA atraviesa antes de llegar al humano.

Guardrail en input y output

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 13 • Originally published at g.cloud

Los guardrails de entrada y salida son mecanismos técnicos que filtran, validan y transforman los datos antes de que un modelo de IA los procese (input) y antes de que sus respuestas sean entregadas al usuario (output), minimizando riesgos de contenido dañino, sesgado o no conforme.

Son controles técnicos integrados en el ciclo de inferencia de modelos de lenguaje. Los guardrails de entrada actúan en tiempo real sobre el prompt recibido: normalizan texto, identifican patrones de ataque (como prompt injection), verifican pertenencia al dominio autorizado y rechazan entradas ambiguas o potencialmente peligrosas. Los guardrails de salida, en cambio, operan tras la generación del texto: clasifican la respuesta según categorías de riesgo (odio, desinformación, privacidad), aplican filtros de tono y precisión, y pueden reescribir o suprimir fragmentos antes de la entrega final. Ambos son componentes esenciales de la arquitectura de seguridad de IA —no son módulos opcionales, sino capas obligatorias en entornos regulados o críticos.

Porque un solo punto de control es insuficiente. Un guardrail de entrada puede fallar ante entradas legítimas pero ambiguas que activan sesgos ocultos del modelo; un guardrail de salida, a su vez, no puede corregir una generación fundamentalmente errónea si no hay supervisión temprana. La combinación permite defensa en profundidad: entrada limpia → generación más predecible → salida auditada. Esto es especialmente relevante en aplicaciones brasileñas donde se requiere alineación con principios constitucionales (dignidad, não discriminação) y normativas sectoriales (ex. Resolução CFM nº 2.287/2021 para IA em saúde).

Con herramientas como IBM Granite Guardrails, que ofrecen APIs para integración nativa con modelos Granite (3.0 y superiores), soporte para reglas personalizables por sector y compatibilidad con pipelines RAG. No requieren reentrenamiento del modelo, sino configuración de políticas basadas en expresiones regulares, modelos ligeros de clasificación y listas de términos bloqueados o permitidos. Su eficacia depende de la calidad de los datos de entrenamiento de los clasificadores auxiliares y de la actualización continua de las reglas frente a nuevas tácticas de evasión.

A: No. Son complementos técnicos: detectan patrones automatizables, pero no sustituyen la evaluación contextual humana ni el juicio ético profesional.

Q: ¿Funcionan igual para todos los modelos de lenguaje?

A: No. Su efectividad varía según la arquitectura, tamaño y dominio del modelo. Requieren ajuste específico para cada caso de uso.

A: No existen normas vinculantes que los exijan per se, pero su adopción está alineada con las diretrizes do CNAI e recomendações da ANPD sobre segurança de sistemas de IA.

Q: ¿Pueden afectar el rendimiento del sistema?

A: Sí, aunque mínimamente: añaden latencia de milisegundos (típicamente <50 ms por capa), compensada por mayor confiabilidad operacional y reducción de incidentes post-despliegue.

U.S. government grounds all flights across the United States ‘due to extraordinary cirumstances’

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The  Federal Aviation Administration said Friday afternoon that it has grounded all flights across the United States due to “extraordinary circumstances” and issues related to safety.

The FAA’s website said the cause is an “equipment outage.” The site itself was down for a brief period at the time of publication, with a note that the website was “experiencing technical difficulties.”

Per an alert sent by the Air Traffic Control System Command Center, or ATCSCC, the FAA said the ground stop means that aircraft cannot take off from departure gates. The message said that the disruption was “due to extraordinary circumstances and for reasons of safety,” but did not specify further. The alert was not visible on the ATCSCC website at press time.

The alert stated that all departing flights must remain on the ground “until further notice.” Inbound international flights will be “managed on a case-by-case basis,” it said.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said in a post on X: “FAA technicians are investigating the issue.”

It’s not clear what caused the ongoing FAA equipment outage. The outage comes in the same week that the administration rolled out an AI-powered tool for air traffic controllers to save aircraft flight time and fuel costs — though it’s unclear if the ongoing outage is related.

A spokesperson for the Federal Aviation Administration did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

FAA Orders Nationwide Ground Stop Due To Air Traffic Control Communication Issues

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

At 3:45 p.m. ET on Friday afternoon, the Federal Aviation Administration issued a nationwide ground stop, halting all flights that were set to take off from U.S. airports until further notice. An ATCSCC advisory was posted by Flightradar24 on Bluesky, which reads: "Due to extraordinary circumstances and for reasons of safety. Attention all aircraft operators, by order of the Federal Aviation Command Center all airports are ground stopped. All departing traffic should remain ground stopped until further notice. Inbound international flights will be managed on a case by case basis."

In a Twitter post, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said, "The FAA issued a nationwide ground stop for all U.S. airports following a communication issue impacting all Air Route Traffic Control Centers. FAA technicians are investigating the issue. We will provide immediate updates as we have them." There's no official word from the FAA yet; the organization has yet to post any statements on social media or its website. According to News9 Oklahoma, reports from aviation monitoring account OSINTdefender said the FAA's ground stop is due to communication frequency issues affecting the National Airspace System. As of right now the ground stop is scheduled to stay in effect until 11:00 p.m. ET, but that could change.

In about as funny a turn of events as you could possibly imagine, our team found out about this situation from a social media post by Flavor Flav, who seems to be one of the first people to post about it. This is a developing story, and we'll update you as we learn more.

A scan of the National Airspace System Status website shows major airports listed as "airport closure," "ground stop," "ground delay," or "departure delay." The last time the FAA enacted a ground stop was in January 2023, when all domestic departures were paused for less than two hours because of a pilot notification system went down. Before that, the last ground stop had been on September 11, 2001. A few days ago we told you about how the FAA is getting rid of ATC's floppy disks, and just yesterday it started rolling out a new AI air traffic tool, though of course there's nothing to suggest that has anything to do with today's issue.

Our very own Andy Kalmowitz is caught up in this mess, as he was about to take off from Chicago O'Hare International Airport on a connecting flight while on his way home from driving the new Subaru Forester Wilderness Hybrid. Right as they were getting ready to taxi the ground stop was issued, and his pilot came over the intercom and explained the situation: "We're in a nationwide ground stop right now. That's not ground delay, that's ground stop. Basically the FAA has said, 'look, we're having radio problems at multiple centers across the nation; it's not just United Airlines, and it's not just Chicago. If you're airborne, that's great, you're coming in to land,' but they are not allowing anyone else to take off and get out there into the system. Which is understandable, but I don't like it, and I know you don't either."

‘I Didn’t Want to Become Fat, Bloated, Naked Elvis’: Why KISS Called It Quits on Touring (And What They’re Up to Now)

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Gene Simmons and Paul Stanley are gearing up for the release of the KISS: End of the Road documentary.

It’s been nearly three years since KISS played its last concert as a touring act — and in makeup. Now the group is giving fans a look behind the scenes of its End of the Road World Tour, a globe-spanning farewell trek that was bumpier than the KISS camp let on. Explore Kiss See latest videos, charts and news

The revealing KISS: End of the Road documentary drops Nov. 19 via KISSOnline.com, where it will be available for seven days before moving on to other platforms. The band will preview the film during its KISS Kruise: Land-Locked in Vegas convention Nov. 13-15 at the Virgin Hotel in Las Vegas, where it will also play acoustic and electric concerts and host a tribute to late original guitarist Ace Frehley, who passed away in October 2025 at the age of 74. Related Aria Vega Wins Best New Artist at the 2026 Latin Grammys: 'Dreams Know No Fear' On Winning First Latin Grammy, Camila Fernández Thanks Expectant Baby for 'Strength to be Here' 50 Cent Trolls Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's Affair With 'P.I.M.P.' Music Video AI Deepfake: 'Jensen Don't Sweat It'

KISS’ recent spate of activity also includes the release of box sets celebrating the 50th anniversary of the group’s fifth studio album, Rock and Roll Over, which spent 45 weeks on the Billboard 200 and peaked at No. 11 in 1976. At the same time, the KISS Army is awaiting an avatar show that’s been in the works for several years, as well as a biopic that’s also in process.

“It’s as it should be,” the group’s Paul Stanley tells Billboard via Zoom about KISS’ continuing life after reaching the End of the Road. “KISS is larger than life, and KISS isn’t about the four individuals. It’s about the four persons, the four characters. It’s a timeless thing, so I totally get those four going on. I can get older, but he [Stanley’s Starchild character] won’t.”

Gene Simmons, whom Stanley founded KISS with during 1973 in New York, adds that, “We’ve seen bands that wait too long to get off the road and it’s sad. I didn’t want to become fat, bloated, naked Elvis on the bathroom floor. So you have to know when to get off the stage as KISS. But that doesn’t mean (the band) has to end.”

The nearly two-and-a-half-hour KISS: End of the Road film shows in intimate detail that the conclusion of that phase of the band’s career was emotional, triumphant and trying.

Directed by DJ Viola, who also helmed the 2021 A&E documentary KISStory, the chronicle focuses on the final 2023 leg of the pandemic-interrupted End of the Road World Tour, which started in South America and played North America, Europe and Australia before wrapping up with homecoming performances Dec. 1-2 at Madison Square Garden in New York City.

Noting that KISStory was “what we call a ‘womb-to-tomb’ documentary,” Viola tells Billboard, “I wanted End of the Road to be almost the antithesis of that approach. Rather than having the guys tell us what happened, I wanted audiences to experience it as it was happening, from the first of their final 50 shows all the way to Madison Square Garden.” He and his crew became embedded flies on the wall during the trek, capturing planning meetings, backstage intrigue and plenty of onstage highlights, as well as interviews with band members, manager Doc McGhee, Simmons’ and Stanley’s wives and others.

“For 50 years,” Viola notes, “KISS has been protective of what happens behind the curtain. Everyone says they want to show the ‘warts and all,’ but very few actually mean it. This time, the band opened that curtain completely. What you discover is that beneath all the makeup, pyro and spectacle is an unwavering commitment to giving their fans the greatest show possible. After 50 years, that dedication hasn’t diminished one bit.”

There are warts aplenty, too, including: production difficulties early on that created significant tension in the touring party; Simmons being briefly incapacitated by dehydration during a concert in Brazil; and Stanley suffering from a severe flu that led to the cancellation of nine shows and put the last stretch of the tour in jeopardy. There are touching scenes as well, with the band and those around them talking about reaching the end of touring life. Stanley and Simmons both choke up during the final concert while talking from the stage about their parents and growing up in New York, and Simmons even shoos the camera crew away as he and Stanley exchange sentiments during that night’s sound check.

The film ends with the two men meeting, post-tour, for a convivial conversation at a deli in Bel Air, Calif.

“The trick to being unobtrusive in documentary filmmaking is to be ubiquitous,” Viola says. “You have to be there for the dullest moments as well as the most exciting ones…. The more you’re around, the more people stop thinking about the camera. Eventually, you’re just another person in the room. You have to become part of the team while remaining as invisible as possible. It’s a strange balance, but that’s where the best moments come from.”

He adds that, “The trust we’d established during KISStory was essential. KISS allowed me into virtually every corner of their world, from rehearsals and travel to putting on their makeup, standing onstage during the shows and being there afterward when the makeup came off and they dissected the night’s performance. They knew I wasn’t there to manufacture controversy. I was there to authentically capture what was happening, good or bad.”

Stanley says the band was not bothered by the constant camera presence, explaining that at this point, he and Simmons are more comfortable letting fans inside aspects of KISS that were previously kept under wraps — and deliberately so.

“It’s more appropriate to do now,” he says. “It’s famously remembered that for the early years of the band, nobody ever saw us without makeup and that was part of the mystique, part of building this larger-than-life persona. At this point, it’s interesting to let people go behind it and in some ways, it makes it bigger because it’s such a Herculean effort to do what we’ve done. To have a band go on stage 50 years later and people go, ‘They look the same as they did,’ well, that doesn’t happen by chance. It’s all very encompassing, and always has been…. I think people can appreciate that now and realize that there’s a real person behind Superman with a Marshall amp.”

The End of the Road film nods to the past with historical footage, and it also provides a glimpse into the future by showing the group working with Sweden’s Pophouse Entertainment — which acquired KISS’ catalog and trademarks for a reported $300 million during 2024 — on the digital avatar show, which was previewed at the final Madison Square Garden date. “They’re great new partners,” Simmons says. “They’re infusing so much money into KISS; you’re gonna have theme parks and cartoons and movies…just some amazing stuff coming. They’re very cognizant of the fact that the same-old isn’t the way to do it… and want to make it easier for fans who want to control the content.”

The KISS avatar show is still a work in progress, but Simmons predicts that it will arrive “in the next year to two years, at a place that is soon to be announced. They’re really putting a lot of effort  into it.” Stanley notes that “it’s far more complicated than I certainly anticipated. It’s quite complex. But the rewards are that much greater, too.” And he clarifies that it will likely be something different from what’s expected.

“The idea was never to replicate a concert. That’s not what this is about,” Stanley says. “It’s to create an experience and almost make us your tour guides into something unworldly, and yet totally based in the music. And the idea was never to do something that was appealing exclusively to a KISS fan; we wanted to create a must-see, so to speak, regardless of your taste in music. That’s what we’re creating.”

The KISS biopic is also moving forward, with Joseph McGinty “McG” Nichol directing and casting. “Anybody who’s ever been involved in film knows you can get old waiting for a film to get made,” Stanley says. “It’s still in motion, and that’s all I can say. It’s very slow going; you can rush something out but, frankly, I love being in a position where if we can’t do a film that I’m proud of, I don’t need to do a film. It just doesn’t matter that much. It would be a nice feather in the cap, but it better be a good-looking feather or I don’t want it.”

Simmons and Stanley may have more to reveal at the KISS Kruise. Both have been preparing for the band’s performances as well as the Frehley tribute, an all-star event during which Frehley’s band will back a roster of guests, including alumnus guitarist Bruce Kulick.

The Rock and Roll Over sets, meanwhile, feature a new mix by original album producer Eddie Kramer, along with outtakes, alternative mixes, demos and a live 1977 performance from Japan. “To have Eddie Kramer go in and rethink things was great,” Stanley says. “And I’m certainly open to looking into other people working on other albums and go in and reimagine them, rebirth them. I’m always in favor of making something better when it’s possible.”

Both men have their own projects as well: Simmons has dates booked into 2027 with his solo band and produced the film Deep Water; Stanley is actively painting “four or five days a week” and says his band Soul Station “will get back up and running soon.”

Meanwhile, KISS is also approaching the one-year anniversary (Dec. 7) of its Kennedy Center Honor — and also potentially being part of the last class to be feted in the actual venue, whose main building is closed to the public amid disputes over U.S. President Donald Trump’s efforts to rename and remodel the historic venue. (This year’s Honors will be presented at the Capital One Arena in Washington, D.C.)

None of that has affected the way Simmons and Stanley view their honor, however.

“All things must pass; nothing lasts forever,” Simmons notes. “I remember when Elvis (Presley) was invited by President Nixon to receive some kind of awards and stuff; I thought, ‘Cool.’ Do I agree with the politics of President Nixon? No, but he’s the duly elected president. President Trump is our president, and that’s it. What you think politically, that’s for the ballot box.”

Stanley adds that, “I don’t believe anyone ever went to any of the (honorees) and said, ‘Who did you vote for?’ That’s supposed to be a non-issue. For me, getting it was just part of the trifecta, so to speak. We got the Rock & Roll Hall of Fame. We got the Songwriters Hall of Fame, and then to get the Kennedy Center Honor…. I’m at a point in my life now where it really comes down to, if you love me, let me know. Don’t put it in an obituary or my eulogy. If there’s an accolade you want to give me, I’m here.”

Zoho CRM telephony: built-in calling, Zoho Voice and PhoneBridge compared

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 13 • Originally published at skyfallhq.net

Disclosure: Skyfall Labs is an official Zoho Partner. This is a cross-post of our guide; the original, with sources, is at https://www.skyfallhq.net/resources/zoho-crm-telephony-phonebridge-built-in-vs-zoho-voice

Short answer: Zoho says PhoneBridge (Zoho Telephony) is not a separate app but an integration platform that connects CRM to either Zoho's built-in telephony, Zoho Voice or another Marketplace phone provider. Built-in telephony runs on Twilio, is set up by an administrator and covers basic calling; Zoho Voice is Zoho's own cloud PBX with more features. Each route has its own trap: Zoho says built-in numbers without a call flow reject incoming calls, and Zoho Voice calls work in CRM only if numbers are assigned to Admin users and Telephony agents in Zoho Voice. Skyfall Labs is an official Zoho Partner and has a commercial interest in Zoho projects; product facts are limited to the cited pages and our advice is labelled as advice.

Zoho's comparison page says built-in telephony fits teams that make few calls, use CRM on the web and do not want a separate PBX app, while Zoho Voice fits teams needing call-centre features, reporting or mobile calling. Zoho's pages describe the extra Zoho Voice features as live calls, reports, business hours, voicemail, spam blocking and free agent-to-agent calling.

Source: Zoho Voice Help: Zoho Voice vs Zoho Telephony

Source: Zoho Help: Configuring built-in telephony in Zoho CRM

Zoho says incoming calls are routed through the call flow associated with the number, and that if a number is not associated with a call flow, incoming calls are rejected for that number. A flow is enabled for its numbers when you click Publish; drafts can be saved and compared with the published version. So a team that bought a number and added users but never published a flow can have working outbound calls and dead inbound ones.

Zoho's built-in page lists no numeric limits for numbers, call flows or recording storage, so we state none.

Source: Zoho Help: Configuring built-in telephony in Zoho CRM

Source: Zoho Voice Help: Integrations - Zoho Telephony

Source: Zoho Voice Help: Integrations - Zoho Telephony

Zoho's comparison page says both options are available in the US and EU data centres, while in AU only built-in telephony is listed, and in IN only built-in with Zoho Voice expected in Q3 2025. That page may be out of date, so check your own data centre in Zoho's current documentation. It also says built-in telephony calls are web-only, so mobile calling needs Zoho Voice or another provider, and that users in regions without Zoho Voice can start with built-in and later port numbers without service disruption. That last point is Zoho's claim and not something we tested.

Source: Zoho Voice Help: Zoho Voice vs Zoho Telephony

Telephony touches licences, permissions, call routing and CRM data in one go. If you are comparing options or inherited a setup where calls are not logging, a short design review usually costs less than trial and error. That review is part of Zoho CRM customization at Skyfall Labs. We have a commercial interest, so weigh our view accordingly.

Zoho says Zoho Telephony (PhoneBridge) is not a separate app but an integration platform that connects CRM to built-in telephony or to a cloud phone provider such as Zoho Voice from the Marketplace.

Why are incoming calls rejected on my built-in Zoho CRM number?

Zoho says that if a number is not associated with a call flow, incoming calls are rejected for that number. Create a call flow, associate the number and publish it.

Why do calls not work for my users after integrating Zoho Voice?

Zoho says calls work in CRM only if you assign phone numbers to Admin users and Telephony agents in Zoho Voice, on the Call Config page.

Zoho says users with Administrator permission set up built-in telephony, and for Zoho Voice (Standard plan) configuration can be done only by Admin users in Zoho Voice.

Per Zoho, you buy credits (1 credit equals 1 USD in local currency) that cover numbers, calls and recording storage upgrades, with monthly charges for numbers. Check Zoho's pricing page for rates, which we do not quote.

Does built-in Zoho CRM telephony work on the mobile app?

Zoho's comparison page says built-in telephony works in web apps only, not the CRM or Bigin mobile apps; Zoho Voice offers web and mobile.

Need hands-on help? Skyfall Labs offers Zoho consulting, implementation and customization. Original guide: https://www.skyfallhq.net/resources/zoho-crm-telephony-phonebridge-built-in-vs-zoho-voice

Your fallback provider is the thing that breaks first

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anyone running an LLM feature for more than a few months has a fallback path: a second provider, a router, or a forty-line wrapper that tries the next model when the first one returns 529 or 503. The design work usually stops at "if the primary fails, use the secondary." That is the easy half.

The hard half is what happens to the secondary at the exact moment the primary trips.

When the primary starts returning overload errors, every client fails over within the same few seconds. The secondary does not receive a trickle; it receives its normal traffic plus the entire primary's traffic, arriving as one burst, with retry loops attached.

If the secondary was sized for its own load plus a small margin, it was sized for a number that never occurs during an incident. The result is a familiar pattern: the primary degrades, the fallback immediately degrades, and the incident looks like "both providers are down" when in fact the second one is being DDoS'd by its own customer.

With a secondary at 30% of primary capacity and a 2x retry multiplier, the fallback sees roughly 2.6x what it handles on a good day, in the first minute.

1. Treat Retry-After as authoritative, and jitter everything else. 503 and 529 carry the header inconsistently, so read it when present, and when absent use exponential backoff with jitter. Unjittered backoff turns a synchronised burst into a synchronised second burst, one interval later.

3. Instrument failover rate per provider, not just error rate. Error rate tells you what is failing now. Failover rate tells you how much of your traffic has already moved, which is the number that predicts whether the fallback is about to become the incident. In my experience it leads the user-visible symptom by minutes, which is the only window where you can act.

A fallback is not a spare tire. It is a second system that has to absorb a correlated spike at exactly the moment its sibling is unhealthy. Designing it as identical-but-idle guarantees it will be the first thing to fall over, and it will do so while every dashboard still shows the primary as the problem.

The cheap version of all this is a single line in the runbook: when the primary trips, cap fallback concurrency before you touch anything else. The expensive version is discovering it during the incident.
