Yiren Digital Receives NYSE Notification Regarding Stock Price Requirement

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

BEIJING, Nov. 13, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Yiren Digital Ltd. (NYSE: YRD) ("Yiren Digital" or the "Company"), a leading company specializing in financial technology and artificial intelligence innovation across multiple industries in China and global markets, today announced that it received a letter dated October 20, 2026, from the New York Stock Exchange (the "NYSE") notifying the Company that it was not in compliance with the NYSE's continued listing standard under Section 802.01C of the NYSE Listed Company Manual (the "Price Criteria") for the average closing price of the Company's American depositary shares (the "ADSs") was below US$1.00 per ADS over a consecutive 30-trading-day period.

The Company has six months following receipt of the notification (the "Cure Period") to regain compliance with the Price Criteria. The Company can regain compliance during the Cure Period if, on the last trading day of any calendar month during that period, its ADSs have both a closing price of at least US$1.00 per ADS and an average closing price of at least US$1.00 per ADS over the 30-trading-day period ending on that day. In the event that at the expiration of the Cure Period, both a $1.00 closing price and a $1.00 average closing price over the preceding 30 trading days are not attained, the NYSE will commence suspension and delisting procedures.

The notification does not immediately affect the Company's business operations. The Company's ADSs will continue to be listed and traded on the NYSE subject to compliance with other applicable continued listing standards. The Company will continue to monitor the trading price of its ADSs and consider available measures to regain compliance.

Yiren Digital Ltd. is a leading company specializing in financial technology and artificial intelligence innovation across multiple industries in China and global markets. The Company leverages advanced artificial intelligence and emerging technologies to enhance customer experience, optimize capital efficiency, and expand financial inclusion. Following the regulatory filing of its in-house developed Large Language Model Zhiyu and the significant enhancement of its MagiCube Agent platform, Yiren Digital is establishing a new growth engine to accelerate its evolution into an AI-native, multi-industry operating platform extending beyond traditional financial services. For more information, please visit https://ir.yiren.com.

Cold Plunges Are Finding a New Place in Residential Design

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Once relegated to backyards and home gyms, cold plunges are finding a new place in residential design. As the wellness practice grows in popularity, homeowners are increasingly looking to incorporate cold therapy directly into their bathrooms, transforming what was once a standalone piece of fitness equipment into an integrated feature of the home.

The interest goes beyond athletic recovery. Cold-water immersion has been associated with potential benefits ranging from reduced muscle soreness to improved sleep and stress management, although research into its broader health effects remains limited. Still, the appeal is evident. According to Houzz’s 2024 Summer Trends Report, searches for “cold plunge” increased 74% year over year, while searches for “indoor sauna” rose 69%.

In the Heber, Utah, residence by Berg Design Studio, Hydro Systems’ Marquis bathtub demonstrates how traditional freestanding tubs can accommodate emerging wellness technologies, including cold plunging. Photography by Steve C. Wilson.

That interest reflects a larger shift toward wellness-focused residential spaces. The company’s 2026 report found that searches for “wellness room” had increased 164%, suggesting that homeowners are thinking more intentionally about how their environments can support relaxation and well-being.

For interior designers, these changing priorities are introducing new considerations into residential projects. Arizona-based designer Stephanie Larsen, who incorporates cold plunges, infrared saunas, and integrated aromatherapy systems into her work, has seen clients place greater emphasis on these features. “Today’s clients are craving homes that heal and inspire,” she told Luxe Interiors + Design. “They want spaces infused with wellness technology.”

Accommodating those requests means thinking beyond individual fixtures to how different wellness experiences function together. Cold plunges, for instance, are often paired with saunas for contrast therapy, which alternates between hot and cold exposure. Bringing both into a residential setting requires careful consideration of available space, plumbing, and the relationship between the different elements. Manufacturers, meanwhile, are developing more integrated systems that offer greater flexibility.

Hydro Systems integrates cold-plunge technology into freestanding bathtubs, bringing cold therapy into residential bathrooms without the need for separate equipment. Photography courtesy of Hydro Systems.

“We’re seeing wellness become a more intentional part of residential design, with dedicated spaces that bring together multiple hydrotherapy experiences in a single tub, from cold plunging and whirlpool to oxygenated microbubble therapy,” says Ashley Steinhardt, VP of Sales at Hydro Systems.

The California-based bath manufacturer has developed its Hydro Chill technology to incorporate cold plunging into customizable bathtubs, eliminating the need for a separate fixture. Its latest iteration also introduces smart-home functionality, allowing users to adjust water temperatures and schedule plunges remotely.

Hydro Systems is also exploring how multiple hydrotherapy experiences can share the same footprint. Its new Hydro Chill Ultra delivers both hot and cold water through a self-contained system that generates its own hot water. Because it doesn’t require a traditional hot-water connection, the technology offers additional flexibility for installations in pool houses and dedicated wellness rooms.

Featured in an Atlanta Homes Showhouse, Hydro Systems’ Slate bathtub features a custom color and polished finish, with integrated Hydro Chill cold-plunge technology. Paired with a wood-paneled sauna, the tub demonstrates how cold therapy can become part of a thoughtfully designed residential wellness space. Photography courtesy of Hydro Systems.

While expansive home spas provide room for separate saunas, plunge tubs, and other amenities, integrating hydrotherapy into an existing bathroom requires a different approach. Multifunctional fixtures can help reduce the space needed for these experiences, making them easier to accommodate within conventional residential layouts.

“Wellness doesn’t have to be confined to a dedicated room,” Steinhardt says. “Hydrotherapy can also be incorporated into smaller bathroom footprints, bringing wellness into everyday routines.”

Hydro Systems’ Hydro Chill cold-plunge system is incorporated into a home gym alongside weight-training equipment, bringing cold-water immersion into a dedicated fitness and recovery space. Photography courtesy of Hydro Systems.

Cold plunges may still be a relatively niche residential amenity, but their growing visibility is prompting designers to reconsider what a bathroom can accommodate. As more wellness features make their way into the home, the challenge will be integrating them without compromising the functionality, comfort, and aesthetics of the spaces around them.

On this day in space! Nov. 13, 1971: NASA's Mariner 9 becomes 1st spacecraft to orbit Mars, revealing close looks at the Martian surface

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Mariner 9 cemented its place in space history when it entered into orbit around Mars, the first human-built spacecraft ever to do so.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On Nov. 13, 1971, Mariner 9 became the first spacecraft to orbit Mars. At the time, scientists didn't know much about the Red Planet.

NASA had several Mars flybys before Mariner 9. Those flybys showed that Mars had a cratered surface, so some scientists thought that Mars looked just like the moon. Then Mariner 9 blew blew their minds.

When Mariner 9 got to Mars, it arrived in the middle of a dust storm. It saw some tall structures sticking through the clouds. When the dust settled, images from Mariner 9 revealed that these structures were the tops of huge volcanoes.

One volcano, Olympus Mons, is the tallest in the solar system. Mariner 9 also spotted an enormous canyon called Valles Marineris. This canyon is five times longer than the Grand Canyon in Arizona.

Mariner 9 worked for one year in orbit. When it ran out of gas, NASA shut it off. But the spacecraft remains one of the most famous Mars voyagers of all time.

Mariner 9 cemented its place in space history when it entered into orbit around Mars, the first human-built spacecraft ever to do so.

The NASA spacecraft actually beat the Soviet Union's own Mars 2 spacecraft to the Red Planet, even though Mars 2 "had an 11-day head start," NASA said in a mission description. (Mars 2 would go on to be the first human-built craft to impact the surface of Mars when it crashed into the Red Planet.)

But it was the science from Mariner 9 that transformed Mars from a mysterious Red Planet into a full world for humans to explore.

Mariner 9 carried four primary instruments to study Mars, including a camera system for imaging, an ultraviolet spectrometer, an infrared spectrometer and an infrared radiometer. The probe mapped 85% of Mars with those tools during its nearly one-year mission.

The Mariner 9 spacecraft discovered 20 giant volcanoes on Mars (including Olympus Mars) and made key observations to map Valles Marineris, which runs 2,500 miles (4,000 kilometers) long acros the Tharsis region and is 120 miles (200 km) wide.

"The canyon was named in honor of Mariner 9," NASA said in an overview.

In all, Mariner 9 took 7,329 photos of the surface of Mars, mapping the planet down to a resolution of between 1/2-1 mile (1-2 km). It also snapped at least 80 photos of Phobos and Deimos, the two small moons of Mars.

By February 1972, Mariner 9's supply of gaseous nitrogen, which it used to feed its attitide control thrusters, had run dry. NASA finally shut down the mission in October 1972.

Want more space history? Check out our full On This Day In Space Story archive and watch our On This Day In Space videos on YouTube.

On this day in space! Nov. 12, 2014: European Philae probe lands on Comet 67P, bounced twice and then got lost for nearly 2 years

Hanneke Weitering is a multimedia journalist in the Pacific Northwest reporting on the future of aviation at FutureFlight.aero and Aviation International News and was previously the Editor for Spaceflight and Astronomy news here at Space.com. As an editor with over 10 years of experience in science journalism she has previously written for Scholastic Classroom Magazines, MedPage Today and The Joint Institute for Computational Sciences at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. After studying physics at the University of Tennessee in her hometown of Knoxville, she earned her graduate degree in Science, Health and Environmental Reporting (SHERP) from New York University. Hanneke joined the Space.com team in 2016 as a staff writer and producer, covering topics including spaceflight and astronomy. She currently lives in Seattle, home of the Space Needle, with her cat and two snakes. In her spare time, Hanneke enjoys exploring the Rocky Mountains, basking in nature and looking for dark skies to gaze at the cosmos.

The UGREEN HomeAgent is a ‘local-first’ way to make your smart home even smarter

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Smart home tech often relies solely on cloud connections and pricey AI subscriptions, but UGREEN is delivering a local-first solution in its new HomeAgent collection, delivering cameras, speakers, and more that bring the bulk of the functionality and processing directly to your home.

The UGREEN AIoT – AI of Things – ecosystem is built around pairing hardware to a central hub that acts as your home’s brain. The HomeAgent HA100 and Pro are that “brain,” pairing to smart home gear such as lights, speakers, and cameras to not only store recordings, but process the data locally.

At the core of the experience is UGREEN’s Uliya AI assistant, which you can talk to directly through HomeAgent HA100 or through a connected Smart Speaker for Uliya. The assistant can handle simple tasks like controlling lights or playing music, of course, but can do a lot more with video recordings. You can ask questions such as “is there a package outside” or “where is my dog” and Uliya will be able to parse through camera recordings to find the answer rather than forcing you to have to scroll through timelines.

That’s all powered by the HomeAgent HA100. It can also acts as network storage for photos, household files, and more, all of which can be used as context for the local AI system – and there’s no monthly fee to store or access it.

UGREEN’s hardware lineup around the HomeAgent centerpieces includes cameras such as the SynCare cameras. These deliver the local recording capabilities that are so central to the system. SynCare OD800 Pro is a triple-lens outdoor camera with solar power and a built-in battery, while the OD600 Pro delivers the same powerful camera setup with power-over-ethernet. These have an 8MP primary camera and dual 5MP cameras with pan-tilt-zoom capabilities. They can zoom in up to 9x, feature motion tracking and a built-in spotlight, as well as color night vision and IP66 dust/water protection.

Meanwhile, the SynCare Indoor Cam ID500 Pro is a pan-tilt-zoom camera meant to be used inside to capture events in an entire room. It features 4K imaging, 360-degree pan and 160-degree vertical tilt while supporting up to 8x digital zoom, motion tracking, and even baby-cry detection. It also has a physical shutter for your privacy.

There are also two smart frames, the UGREEN Gallery E-Paper Smart Frame mounts on the wall to display photos and artwork, while the UGREEN Gallery LCD Smart Frame can sit on a table or hang on the wall, showing photos and videos, while also integrating with the rest of your smart home’s functions.

UGREEN has its own smart speaker for the system, and you can also integrate select Matter hardware as well, including smart lighting, robot vacuums and other smart home devices.

The UGREEN HomeAgent ecosystem will start at $799 for a limited time when its Kickstarter campaign launches on April 13, 2027, with upgraded packages that drastically increase AI processing power, storage, and the types of devices you can connect to the system.

Mirendil raises $1B in funding to speed up AI model development

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mirendil Inc., a provider of software that speeds up artificial intelligence development, has raised $1 billion in early-stage funding.

The startup stated on Thursday that Kleiner Perkins and Clearlake Capital jointly led the Series A investment. They were joined by more than a half-dozen others including Google LLC.

Mirendil was launched last year by two former Google DeepMind and Anthropic PBC researchers. Chief Executive Officer Behnam Neyshabur is a co-inventor of SAM, a popular algorithm that makes AI models better at tasks they were not specifically trained to perform. Chief Technology Officer Harsh Mehta, in turn, spent his time at Google DeepMind and Anthropic working on pretraining projects. Pretraining is a term for the initial phase of the AI development workflow.

Mirendil’s first product, Lor, is a platform that automates many of the tasks involved in model training runs. The company says that the software does so by splitting work among hundreds of AI agents.

Large language model development involves a significant amount of trial and error. Researchers create multiple versions of an algorithm that differ from one another in small ways and compare them to find the most performant variant. According to Mirendil, Lor can automate that workflow. If an error crops up during the evaluation, the platform fixes it automatically.

Earlier this year, Mirendil asked one of its researchers to build a coding model that can compete with leading open-source LLMs. Usually, such projects are carried out by teams of specialists. Mirendil says that its automation technology enabled the researcher to build the model without any help from colleagues. The project cost $10 million and took about 3.5 months to complete.

The company’s agents wrote more than 99% of the code that powers the model, which is called Mirendil-Base. They set up infrastructure, found a suitable training algorithm and collected training data from the web. The agents then refined both the training dataset and Mirendil-Base’s architecture to boost output quality.

The LLM is the foundation of a specialized coding model called Fern that the company unveiled last month. According to Mirendil, it’s designed to automate programming challenges commonly encountered by AI labs. Fern can process 10 million input tokens per prompt, or 10 times more than Claude Opus 5.5. That’s the equivalent of about a half-million lines of code.

Fern is based on a version of Mirendil-Base with 100 billion to 500 billion parameters. Late last month, the company previewed two upgraded versions of the latter model with between 500 billion and 10 trillion parameters. Mirendil plans to use those algorithms to develop more capable LLMs.

Why Viruses Make You Sneeze: COVID Proteins Directly Hijack Sensory Nerves

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Neuroscientists have discovered that SARS-CoV-2 viral proteins directly activate pain-sensing nerves in the airways to trigger sneezing, coughing, and body aches, overturning the long-held view that symptoms stem solely from immune inflammation.

The study shows that the viral enzyme PLpro activates the wasabi-sensing TRPA1 receptor on sensory neurons, offering new therapeutic targets for acute viral symptoms and long COVID.

For decades, textbooks have explained the misery of respiratory infections, the racking coughs, relentless sneezing, headaches, and full-body aches, as collateral damage from the immune system. According to conventional wisdom, infected airway cells and responding immune cells release a storm of inflammatory mediators that secondarily irritate nearby nerve endings.

Now, a study published in the journal Neuron reveals that coronaviruses do not wait for the immune system to sound the alarm; their viral proteins directly hijack the peripheral nervous system.

Led by Dr. Diana Bautista, professor of molecular and cell biology at UC Berkeley, member of the Helen Wills Neuroscience Institute, and a Howard Hughes Medical Institute (HHMI) investigator, the research demonstrates that specific SARS-CoV-2 enzymes directly stimulate airway sensory neurons, triggering immediate sneezing, coughing, airway inflammation, and heightened pain sensitivity.

When SARS-CoV-2 infects host cells, it manufactures two specialized enzymes, papain-like protease (PLpro) and main protease (Mpro), to slice long viral polyproteins into functional components needed for viral replication. Once the virus kills the host cell, these viral proteases spill into the surrounding airway tissue, coming into direct contact with specialized pain- and irritant-sensing neurons known as nociceptors.

Bautista, who previously discovered that the TRPA1 ion channel acts as the sensory receptor for pungent chemicals in wasabi and garlic, found that SARS-CoV-2 hijacks this exact molecular pathway:

To test how PLpro behaves in a living organism, Bautista’s team applied just two microliters of purified PLpro to the nostrils of mice. While plain water occasionally elicited a single sneeze, PLpro triggered explosive bouts of up to 30 sneezes per minute, alongside heightened mechanical pain sensitivity.

Convincing peer reviewers that mice genuinely sneeze required rigorous physiological proof. The team acquired a specialized $75,000 plethysmograph to record the characteristic respiratory lung spasms accompanying each sneeze, matching patterns documented in other sneezing mammals, and dyed the nasal fluid blue to measure the expelled aerosol volume.

While antiviral medications like Paxlovid target Mpro to halt viral replication, numerous experimental inhibitors targeting PLpro were developed during the pandemic without yet reaching clinical approval.

“We think that these protease inhibitors that people spent a lot of time and money developing could be important not just for preventing viral replication,” said Bautista. “They could also help treat symptoms like coughing, which could limit viral spread, and potentially prevent severe COVID-19 in individuals who are susceptible.”

Furthermore, direct viral sensitization of nociceptors offers a compelling biological mechanism for chronic neurological and respiratory complaints in long COVID—including persistent cough, shortness of breath, chest pain, and headaches.

“There are many modes by which viral and bacterial infections can act in our bodies — there are distinct molecular and cellular routes of triggering this type of airway hyper-reactivity,” Bautista noted. “So far, it looks like they can drive symptoms through both the immune system and the nervous system, using different molecular mechanisms.”

How does SARS-CoV-2 cause sneezing, coughing, and body aches so quickly?

Researchers discovered that when virus-infected cells die, they release viral enzymes, specifically papain-like protease (PLpro) and main protease (Mpro), that directly stimulate pain-sensing nerves (nociceptors) in the airways, triggering immediate sneezing, coughing, and pain rather than waiting solely for immune inflammation.

What is the TRPA1 receptor, and what does it have to do with COVID-19?

TRPA1 is an ion channel on sensory nerves famously known as the “wasabi receptor” because it detects irritating chemicals in wasabi and garlic. The study found that SARS-CoV-2’s PLpro enzyme directly activates TRPA1 on airway nerves, causing the release of neuropeptides that trigger coughing, sneezing, and pain.

Could these findings lead to new treatments for COVID-19 or long COVID?

Yes. Blocking the PLpro enzyme with targeted protease inhibitors could calm overactive sensory nerves, helping to stop severe coughing, reduce airborne viral spread, and potentially alleviate chronic neuropathic symptoms like persistent cough, chest pain, and headaches seen in long COVID.

Funding: The work was funded by grants from the National Institutes of Health (OD TR01 NS116992) and the Howard Hughes Medical Institute. Co-authors of the paper are Sonali Mali, Ricardo Silva, Zhongyan Gong, Michael Cronce, Uyen Vo, Cliff Vuong and Jeffery Cox of UC Berkeley and Yalda Moayedi of the New York University College of Dentistry. Bautista is a member of Berkeley’s Helen Wills Neuroscience Institute.

A coronavirus protease activates sensory neurons via TRPA1 to drive sneeze, cough, and pain

Sneezing, coughing, and pain are common symptoms of respiratory viral infection. These responses are initiated by airway-innervating nociceptors that detect noxious stimuli, but how SARS-CoV-2 activates these neurons remains unclear.

Here, we show that the coronavirus papain-like protease (PLpro) directly activates airway-innervating nociceptors in mice and human induced pluripotent stem cell (iPSC)-derived nociceptors. PLpro triggers sneezing, coughing, acute pain, and calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) release in the airways.

Both the proteolytic activity of PLpro and the host ion channel transient receptor potential ankyrin 1 (TRPA1) are required for neuronal activation and sneezing. Infection with the mouse-adapted SARS-CoV-2 or the murine coronavirus MHV-A59 also induces sneezing in mice, and MHV-A59-evoked sneezing is TRPA1-dependent. SARS-CoV-2 infection of the trigeminal ganglia altered transcriptional programs associated with nociception, including upregulation of the TRPA1-CGRP signaling pathway activated by PLpro.

Together, these findings reveal a direct mechanism by which SARS-CoV-2 triggers pain and airway reflexes and identify PLpro and TRPA1 as potential therapeutic targets for COVID-19.

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The fallout from the shocking news of James Gunn and Peter Safran's departure from DC Studios continues. The duo were brought in to shape the DC cinematic universe into a franchise capable of challenging Disney's MCU for box office supremacy, but they've been dismissed four years after coming in by new management at the newly merged Warner Bros-Paramount-Skydance entity. However, DC fans are already voicing their objection to the changes with a new petition on Change.org.

This isn't the first time DC fans have vented their collective spleen on the internet after C-suite changes. When Zack Snyder was removed from the production of Justice League, and replaced with Joss Whedon, a great hue and cry came from the DC faithful who had enjoyed Snyder's dark takes on Man of Steel and Batman v Superman: Dawn of Justice. Amid a number of box-office and creative struggles, Warner Bros. returned the film to Snyder for Zack Snyder's Justice League, the so-called "Snyder Cut" that restored creative control to Snyder and ballooned the film's length to a hefty 242 minutes. Despite this, occasional calls to "restore the Snyderverse" have gone unheeded.

Now, it's the turn of fans who enjoyed Gunn's sunnier take on the DC universe, as seen in last summer's Superman; they've filed their own petition demanding his reinstatement alongside Safran. The petition argues that Gunn's take on the DC mythos is worth preserving. It also contends that DC Studios, which has already seen one potential shared universe arrive stillborn with 2011's Green Lantern, and another die an undignified death with the conclusion of the pre-Gunn DCEU, needs to maintain some consistency now, and would be harmed by yet another big-screen reboot. As of Friday, November 13, the number of signatures on the petition has reached 70,000. The petition can be viewed and signed at Change.org.

Four teams, four completely different reasons to put yourself in danger for strangers. This is the roster you actually belong on, what they would want from you, and what it would cost you to stay.

You belong where the stakes are largest and the personalities are loudest. This is a team of people who are all used to being the most important person in the room, which is exactly why it works and exactly why it keeps nearly falling apart.

You have never had the luxury of being thanked for this. The X-Men protect a world that has never once been comfortable with them existing, and something in you understands exactly why they keep doing it anyway.

You do not want colleagues, you want your people. The Titans are the team where the friendship came first and the world-saving turned up afterwards as an inconvenient side effect.

You are not asking who to trust. You worked out the answer a while ago and it was nobody in a cape. This is where you go once you have decided the people in charge need watching, and that nobody else is going to do it.

Although plans can clearly change, the immediate future seems certain for the DCU, which will now be spearheaded by former MCU executive Jeremy Latcham. Man of Tomorrow, the follow-up to Superman that is written and directed by Gunn, will pit Superman (David Corenswet) and nemesis Lex Luthor (Nicholas Hoult) against the alien menace of Brainiac (Lars Eidinger); it is set to hit theaters on July 9, 2027. The mockumentary series The People v. Gorilla Grodd and the second season of the animated Creature Commandos are will into production, and likely to be released in the near future; after that, the future is hidden behind the Source Wall, but Latcham will reportedly prioritize projects featuring DC's big-name characters, including Batman and Wonder Woman.

New report claims TikTok Live is fastest-growing platform for game streaming

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As TwitchCon descends upon San Diego, a different hub for video game streaming is celebrating its ascendance. TikTok Live is the fastest growing platform in that category, according to a new report published by the GameSquare subsidiary Hatchet.

Hatchet (previously known as Stream Hatchet) dispensed its TikTok Live status report as part of a biannual update related to the ByteDance-owned platform. During the first half of 2026, gaming streams on TikTok Live pulled in 2.5 billion hours of watch time. That traffic gave TikTok Live a 14% share of the game streaming market, pulling it even with the Stake.com-affiliated platform Kick.

Previous Hatchet reports have characterized Twitch and YouTube Gaming‘s dominant positions within the streaming landscape, but the firm’s latest dispatches have shown that TikTok Live is gaining on its market-leading rivals. While TikTok Live watch time grew 96% year-over-year, Twitch and YouTube Gaming’s respective market shares both went down.

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

Through properties like Hatchet, FaZe Clan, and TubeBuddy, GameSquare has made deep inroads in digital gaming communities. “Hatchet’s latest report shows just how quickly TikTok Live has established itself in gaming live-streaming,” said GameSquare CEO Justin Kenna in a statement. “Nearly doubling its gaming hours watched in a year is a clear signal that audience habits are shifting toward mobile, discovery-driven viewing.”

Kenna cited titles like Roblox, Garena Free Fire, and Mobile Legends as three major drivers of TikTok Live’s watch time uptick. Roblox led all games in terms of TikTok Live watch time during the first half of 2026; it generated 631 million hours of TikTok viewership during that period.

Roblox is a favorite of Gen Alpha gamers, while Free Fire and Mobile Legends appeal to gamers on mobile devices, especially outside of the United States. Those two data points help explain how TikTok Live has managed such a sizable gaming glow-up — it has volume none of its competitors can match. With 1.4 million creators active each day, TikTok Live’s community is nearly twice as big as Twitch’s.

That massive quantity has made TikTok Live a market leader across multiple streaming categories. Its gaming operation, however, is one of its strongest fortes, and rival platforms should be prepared to see more growth from their ByteDance-owned competitor.

Not only did it not get a price cut, it got several price increases

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Somehow, some way, the PS5 has been an extraordinary sales success despite launching in the middle of a global pandemic that caused massive supply chain issues and continuing on today amidst an unprecedented memory shortage and economic headwinds driving hardware prices sky high. Despite it all, people are buying PS5s, and many of them.

Sony confirmed just this week that PS5 has hit an incredible milestone of 100 million units sold in its six years on the market. That's not quite as fast as the PS2 or PS4, but it's a much more impressive achievement when you consider one crucial difference between the current-gen PlayStation and literally every other generation before it. Typically, games consoles decrease in price throughout their life cycles, but because of the disadvantages outlined above, the PS5 has only gotten more expensive, right alongside its competitors, the Xbox Series X/S and Nintendo Switch 2.

So, even though the PS5 failed to hit 100 million units sold faster than its predecessors, it still reached the record for units sold without any sort of permanent price cut from Sony. And before you head to the comments section to point out that you got a PS5 at a discount for Black Friday one year, we're talking about official, permanent price decreases from Sony, not temporary sales from retailers. That is seriously impressive considering the economic difficulties not only Sony and other hardware manufacturers are grappling with today, but consumers as well.

You could almost, almost make the case for PS5 being the first console period to hit that sales milestone without a price cut, but for the mighty Nintendo Switch. The Switch hit 100 million units sold in December 2021, and Nintendo never lowered its price globally. There was a price cut in Europe in September 2021, notably just before the Switch reached 100 million units, but it doesn't feel fair disqualifying it from claiming Sony's title for a small, regional price decrease alone.

Chinese AI labs are training models on Nvidia's Blackwell despite export controls, says ex-OpenAI engineer

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Forensic report finds Blackwell's fingerprint in DeepSeek, Moonshot AI, MiniMax and Ant Group models

US export controls are not stopping AI labs in China from using restricted Nvidia chips, according to a report from a former OpenAI engineer.

Will DePue, who left the AI giant earlier this year to launch Unemployment Labs, an AI research consultancy, published forensic evidence showing that "DeepSeek, Moonshot AI, MiniMax and Ant Group have gained access to Nvidia's Blackwell GPUs, despite restrictions on exporting these chips to China, and are using that hardware to create their AI models."

"This sounds insane, but I discovered that almost every AI model carries a hidden signature of the GPU that trained it," said DePue in a social media post. "When a model is first set up, the starting values of its weights are laid out in a pattern that depends on the exact GPU. Each combination of GPU and seed number produces a unique pattern – a sort of fingerprint of the hardware used to create the model. Traces of that pattern survive training into the published model."

It doesn't sound that insane. The resignation of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang followed from revelations that he had an affair with Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang, who, in 2023, founded Singapore-based Megaspeed International. That company is reportedly under investigation by US authorities for allegedly smuggling Nvidia chips to China.

Huang's departure coincides with a renewed push to tighten US chip export controls. House Speaker Mike Johnson supports the idea. And there are at least four bills awaiting consideration that aim to limit China's access to leading edge AI tech: the AI Overwatch Act, the Chip Security Act, Match Act, and the Cloud Security Act.

On Monday, US Senators Jim Banks (R-Ind.) and Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) cited Jensen Huang's "relationship with a suspected Chinese spy" as a justification for passing the AI Overwatch Act, which would codify the Trump administration's existing chip export controls. The Trump administration on Thursday said it's not sure whether Alice Huang was a spy, an allegation denied by Beijing and currently under investigation by the FBI.

Whatever the case, the current rules clearly are not working. Only a few weeks ago, Lava, an Israel-based security company, said it had found "32 Nvidia B300 GPUs, part of Nvidia's Blackwell Ultra generation, through publicly exposed monitoring endpoints with IP addresses geolocated to China."

These GPUs, Lava noted, are subject to US export controls [PDF]. The security biz said its findings followed from the exposure of monitoring systems intended for internal infrastructure management that were publicly exposed.

The analysis published through DePue's consultancy, "Top Chinese Frontier AI Models Carry the Fingerprint of Export-Controlled Nvidia Blackwell GPUs," takes a different approach: analyzing the published weights of various models from Chinese AI labs.

As described in the report, model weights – the parameters that change in relation to training data – must be initialized with some value. One method for doing so involves a pseudorandom number generation (PRNG) algorithm called Philox. The algorithm starts with a seed value that produces the same set of initial values whenever it's run.

Using PyTorch, initialization can be done by creating multiple GPU threads that work in parallel, with the help of Nvidia's cuRAND library. And because this process is not truly random, it's possible to select a chip and a seed value, generate the initial weights for that hardware and compare that pattern to a model's published weights.

"I searched more than six trillion combinations of chip layout and seed number and matched them to open-weights models," said DePue. "In doing so, I found conclusive evidence that six Chinese AI models – including DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Kimi K3, and MiniMax-M3 – were initialized on Nvidia Blackwell systems. Four of the models match Grace Blackwell, the GB200 and GB300 parts in Nvidia's flagship liquid-cooled rack-scale systems."

Noting that this is the first time MiniMax and Ant Group have been publicly linked to usage of Nvidia's Blackwell, he also cited several limitations to his approach. One is that usage of Blackwell GPUs doesn't prove where the GPUs were located, so it's possible some of these Chinese labs used this hardware through US providers – a loophole the Cloud Security Act aims to close.

DePue's report includes initial seed values that linked DeepSeek-V3 to Nvidia's Hopper and DeepSeek-V4-Flash to Blackwell, for those who wish to attempt to reproduce the results. DuPue claims that multiple senior researchers, both independent and inside frontier AI labs like OpenAI and Anthropic, have confirmed the result.

Coincidentally, OpenAI and Anthropic have each complained about Chinese labs copying their models through distillation, a pastime also noted by the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency. ®

Microsoft 'deeply sorry' for global outage after almost 24 hours of disruption

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Products such as Microsoft Teams and Outlook have been out of service for much of Friday

Microsoft has said it is "deeply sorry" for the service outage to its products, including Outlook and Teams, which has caused disruption around the world.

Services were broadly recovering by Friday afternoon, nearly 24 hours after issues began. Microsoft said it was making "encouraging progress", but added it does not know when services will fully recover.

The outage hit more than 70 countries, disrupting everything from business operations to government services and personal users. More than 430 million people use the affected products worldwide.

Microsoft said it was a technical issue introduced during a "network capacity upgrade" and not related to cyber or artificial intelligence agents.

Experts said the event was a "stark reminder" of how heavily businesses and vital services rely on a small group of big tech providers.

Users began reporting they were having trouble accessing Microsoft 365 products on Thursday.

By Friday, the far-reaching issue was affecting institutions from the Japanese government and the Philippines Supreme Court to the Australian spy agency, and trains, hospitals and courts in North America.

Why is Microsoft's outage lasting so long and will emails be lost?

Courts, spies and MPs: How the Microsoft outage has been felt around the world

In the UK, some parts of the NHS, including GPs and hospitals, were affected, as were some schools and police forces.

UK cyber minister Liz Lloyd said the incident had "limited" impact on critical frontline services such as 999 calls, and there had been no disruption to the transport network.

In Canada, however, the national rail network said passengers may not be able to receive email updates about their trips, and hospitals across the country also had issues with emails.

In the US, staff members from the House of Representatives told Politico it had set back an entire day of legislative work as everything was on Microsoft's Outlook.

Singapore's government acknowledged some of its systems had been impacted, and in India, the outage disrupted work for many on Friday.

In Turkey, advisers and officials attending the COP31 climate summit in Antalya were left frustrated by being unable to access Outlook.

The issues also affected smaller businesses and individuals.

Pete says his business has been brought to a standstill

Peter Pentz, a winemaker in South Africa, said his entire business was basically brought to a "standstill".

His winery's communication with exports was also brought to a standstill, he said, and "critical production work" was been halted as the Cape West Coast winery "relies on these systems for parts of our day-to-day operations".

Georgia from Staffordshire in England said she was due to complete her first house purchase today after waiting eight months to get to this point - but the outage has delayed that.

"The solicitor mentioned there may be a delay due to not being able to contact other agencies.

"We're not the only one completing today, there are hundreds of houses and for them to ring everyone in time is unfeasible," Georgia said.

Microsoft first acknowledged users in the Americas were unable to access Exchange Online mailboxes at 17:54 GMT on Thursday, and shortly after warned the disruption was possibly affecting Microsoft 365 globally.

While it launched an investigation and engineers began working to restore services, by 04:36 on Friday Microsoft was reporting only a "partial recovery" in some parts of the world.

By 13:32, the tech giant said there were "broader improvements" in North America, "sustained improvements" across much of Europe and "encouraging recovery signals" in the Asia Pacific region.

Microsoft said it restored services gradually due to the scale of the problem and to help ensure services remain stable once they are up and running again.

"We are deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners," a Microsoft spokesperson said.

"We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously."

Microsoft said it has no "ETA" for when services will be fully recovered.

A full post-incident report will be released once Microsoft has completed a review.

Experts said while workers may have been happy to not have email access for the day, the incident showed the dependence many organisations have on these cloud-based services.

"The global Microsoft outage is a stark reminder of how heavily we rely on a handful of big tech providers," said Rob Demain, chief executive of cybersecurity firm e2e-assure.

Matt Saunders, field chief technology officer at tech consultancy Adaptavist, said it would be challenging to mitigate against a similar problem.

"Classic IT disaster planning strategies suggest using a second service for resilience, but in practice, it's almost impossible for a business to function without tools such as Microsoft," he said.

"In the wake of this outage, organisations must have a plan in place for how to work without such tools, for when incidents like this occur."

XRP Backer Counters SWIFT’s New Speed Report

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Home » Ripple (XRP) » XRP Backer Counters SWIFT’s New Speed Report

The preference for nearly-immediate settlement time windows unites a roster of banks in SWIFT’s chain trial.

A Swift report highlighting faster cross-border payments drew a pointed response from Digital Asset Investor, who replied that he prefers 3-4 seconds and pointed to XRP.

The exchange underscores the ongoing contrast between improvements in traditional interbank messaging and the settlement times associated with blockchain-based rails.

Swift’s Spotlight on Speed report, released this week, shows that 50% of payments on its network now reach the recipient institution within 40 seconds, with 75% arriving within 10 minutes.

What will define the next generation of cross-border payments?The answer? More than just speed.Throughout @Sibos, conversations repeatedly focused on the customer experience. How do we make cross-border transactions more intuitive, more transparent and more predictable for… pic.twitter.com/yZoeZMDN4z

The cooperative noted that these figures put the industry ahead of the G20’s target for one-hour end-to-end processing by 2027 in the interbank leg of the journey.

The same report emphasizes that the “last mile”, the period after a payment reaches the receiving bank and before it is credited to the beneficiary’s account, still accounts for about 80% of the average end-to-end journey time.

Across 40 receiving markets analyzed, median last-mile processing sat near one hour in roughly one-third of cases, with wide variation between cross-border markets.

The conglomerate has called for greater use of one-leg-out models that connect international payments to domestic instant systems to reduce those final delays.

Digital Asset Investor’s reply to the Swift post was brief: “I prefer 3-4 seconds. XRP.” The comment references the XRP Ledger’s typical ledger close time of roughly 3-5 seconds, which provides finality for on-ledger transfers.

I prefer 3-4 seconds. XRP. https://t.co/tBqXtEsqeM

In Ripple’s On-Demand Liquidity (ODL) model, XRP is used as a bridge asset so that currency conversion and settlement can occur without pre-funded nostro accounts.

End-to-end ODL flows that include exchange conversions have been described in the 3-5 second range for the core ledger step, though real-world completion including compliance and local payouts is often measured in seconds to a few minutes depending on the corridor.

The two claims are not measuring identical segments of a payment. Swift’s 40-second figure tracks time to the recipient institution, while the longer last-mile delays occur after that point.

https://t.co/ZA3CcggMOf @swiftcommunity @XRP @realfrandeolza @Archie_XRPL

XRP’s cited 3-4 second window refers primarily to ledger settlement of the bridge asset itself.

Traditional systems still handle the bulk of global bank-to-bank volume and benefit from established compliance and messaging standards; blockchain rails emphasize reduced pre-funding and faster finality on the asset transfer.

Whether the shorter settlement window on XRP produces a meaningfully better end-user experience continues to depend on how the on- and off-ramps, local banking rails, and compliance steps are integrated in any given corridor.

10 milestones in Nvidia's growth, from the NV1 to $3 trillion in market value

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

This Nvidia timeline traces 10 milestones in the chipmaker's rise, from its 1993 founding and GeForce graphics cards to CUDA, AI data centers and trillion-dollar market value

Nvidia $NVDA started in April 1993 in Santa Clara, California, as a company building 3D graphics for PCs. The founders were Jensen Huang, Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem. On June 18, 2024, it overtook Microsoft $MSFT as the most valuable listed company. Company rankings by market value change daily. That makes the June 2024 milestone a dated one, not a permanent title. The two dates sit 31 years apart.

The link between those two dates is the graphics chip itself. Hardware designed to draw 3D scenes for games turned out to suit other kinds of computing work. Nvidia's CUDA software, introduced in 2006, let developers use its GPUs for general-purpose programs. In 2012, AlexNet showed that the same chips could speed up deep learning. Those two steps pointed the company toward AI systems for data centers. After OpenAI released ChatGPT in November 2022, demand for Nvidia's data center chips rose.

Not every step moved the company forward. Its first product, released in 1995, did not succeed commercially. The company changed its approach to graphics afterward. A planned acquisition was abandoned after regulators raised objections. Its purchase of Mellanox, by contrast, added data center networking technology. Those setbacks and deals sit in the timeline alongside product launches and market value figures.

This Nvidia timeline covers 10 milestones, from the 1993 founding to the June 2024 day the chipmaker passed Microsoft in market value. It is a selection, not a complete history of the company. The entries run in chronological order and are not ranked. The early entries cover graphics chips for PCs and a game console. The middle entries cover the software and research that opened GPUs to new uses. The later entries cover data center hardware, acquisitions, export restrictions and the market value steps that followed. Market value claims in the timeline are tied to the dates they occurred. Each entry stands on its own, with the date, product, deal or figure that defined it.

Nvidia $NVDA was founded in April 1993 by three co-founders: Jensen Huang, Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem. The company was based in Santa Clara, California.

Its early work centered on 3D graphics for personal computers.

The NV1 was Nvidia $NVDA's first product, and it reached the market in 1995. It did not succeed commercially.

The chip arrived about two years after the company's founding. It was Nvidia's first attempt to sell graphics hardware to buyers.

That attempt did not catch on. The commercial failure led Nvidia to change its approach to graphics.

Nvidia $NVDA went public on Nasdaq $NDAQ on January 22, 1999, at $12 a share. In October of that year, it launched the GeForce 256 and marketed the chip as the first GPU, or graphics processing unit.

The listing came less than six years after the company's April 1993 founding. Once its shares began trading on Nasdaq, Nvidia became a publicly traded company.

The product milestone followed about nine months after the listing. In October 1999, Nvidia released the GeForce 256, a graphics chip for PCs.

Nvidia promoted the GeForce 256 as the first GPU. That label was the company's own marketing claim. Nvidia used the term to present the chip as a new kind of graphics product.

The two 1999 events differed in kind. The IPO changed how Nvidia was owned and financed. The GeForce 256 gave the company a new chip and a name for the category it claimed that chip started.

Nvidia $NVDA supplied the graphics processor for Microsoft $MSFT's original Xbox, the game console that launched in 2001. The deal added a console business to the chipmaker's existing sales of graphics cards for PCs.

With the Xbox, Nvidia's graphics hardware reached players through two kinds of products. One was a graphics card installed in a personal computer. The other was a home console sold under Microsoft's brand.

Nvidia $NVDA announced CUDA in November 2006. The platform lets developers run general-purpose programs on Nvidia GPUs, not only graphics code.

The announcement came alongside the GeForce 8800 GTX, a graphics card built on Nvidia's G80 chip. Nvidia introduced the software platform and the new card at the same time.

A GPU had been designed to render images for games. CUDA gave programmers a way to write software that put an Nvidia GPU to work on tasks other than drawing a picture on a screen. That widened the range of jobs the company's hardware could take on.

The GeForce 8800 GTX was a graphics card for PC gaming. Paired with CUDA, the launch addressed two audiences. One was buyers looking for a graphics card. The other was developers who wanted to use a GPU for their own programs.

CUDA's role grew in the years after 2006. It later became the software base for much of AI computing.

That later role traces back to the November 2006 announcement. It marked the point when Nvidia began offering its GPUs for general-purpose computing as well as for graphics.

AlexNet, a neural network built by Alex Krizhevsky, Ilya Sutskever and Geoffrey Hinton, won the 2012 ImageNet competition. The three researchers trained it on two Nvidia $NVDA GTX 580 GPUs.

AlexNet was a deep learning model. Its ImageNet result showed that GPUs could speed up that kind of work.

The 2012 win linked Nvidia's graphics processors to deep learning research. The link came through the work of Krizhevsky, Sutskever and Hinton, who chose a pair of the chipmaker's GPUs to train their network.

In April 2016, Nvidia $NVDA announced the Tesla $TSLA P100, a GPU built on its Pascal architecture. That same month, it introduced the DGX-1, an AI system. Together, the two launches marked the chipmaker's turn toward the data center.

The two products sat at different levels. The Tesla P100 was a single chip. The DGX-1 was a complete system built for AI work. With it, Nvidia offered a finished machine as well as the processors that go inside one.

In August 2016, Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang delivered a DGX-1 to OpenAI. The handover came four months after the system's introduction. It put one of the new machines in the hands of an organization working on artificial intelligence.

Pascal did not stay Nvidia's newest design for long. In 2017, the GPU maker followed it with Volta, an architecture that added tensor cores.

Nvidia $NVDA agreed in March 2019 to buy Mellanox for $6.9 billion. The purchase closed in April 2020 and gave the chipmaker data center networking technology.

About 13 months passed between the announcement and the close. The deal took a business built around graphics processors into the networking side of the data center.

A much larger bid followed. In September 2020, five months after the Mellanox purchase closed, Nvidia announced a $40 billion deal to buy Arm.

That agreement never closed. It stayed pending for about 17 months. In February 2022, the companies abandoned it after regulators raised objections.

The two deals ended differently. Mellanox became part of Nvidia, and its networking technology stayed with the chipmaker. The Arm bid ended without Nvidia acquiring the company.

Nvidia $NVDA announced the H100, a data center GPU built on its Hopper architecture, in March 2022. Demand for Nvidia's data center chips rose after OpenAI released ChatGPT in November 2022.

Hopper is the name of the architecture. H100 is the name of the data center GPU built on it. Nvidia announced the chip in March, before ChatGPT reached the public that November.

ChatGPT was an OpenAI product, not an Nvidia one. The demand that rose after its release was for Nvidia's data center chips. The H100 belongs to that category.

U.S. export rules also limited where those chips could be sold. In October 2022, the U.S. imposed restrictions on exports of advanced chips to China. The rules covered the A100 and the H100, two of Nvidia's data center GPUs.

The H100 therefore came under U.S. export restrictions for China in the same year Nvidia announced it.

Nvidia followed Hopper with another architecture. In March 2024, the company announced Blackwell.

This stretch of Nvidia history runs from the H100 announcement in March 2022 to the Blackwell announcement in March 2024. The October 2022 export restrictions and the November 2022 release of ChatGPT fall between those two dates.

Nvidia $NVDA's market value first passed $1 trillion in May 2023. It crossed $2 trillion in February 2024 and $3 trillion in June 2024. On June 18, 2024, the chipmaker overtook Microsoft $MSFT as the most valuable listed company.

Market value, also called market capitalization, is a company's share price multiplied by its number of shares outstanding. It moves with the stock on every trading day. Each threshold records how investors priced Nvidia on a given date. It is not a fixed measure of the business.

Market value is also separate from a company's sales or profit. A higher market value does not mean a company sold more or earned more than another. It reflects the price investors paid for its shares, multiplied across all of them. When Nvidia passed Microsoft, the comparison was between two stock market valuations on the same day.

The word "listed" matters in that comparison. The ranking covers companies whose shares trade on public stock exchanges. A privately held company has no daily share price. It cannot be measured on the same daily basis.

The two trillion-dollar steps took different lengths of time. Nvidia needed about nine months to go from $1 trillion in May 2023 to $2 trillion in February 2024. The climb from $2 trillion to $3 trillion took about four months and ended in June 2024. Each step added the same $1 trillion in market value. The second step took less than half as long as the first.

The Microsoft milestone came in the same month as the $3 trillion crossing. The lead was a position held on a particular trading day. A company that ranks first on one day can fall behind on the next as share prices move.

The dates mark first crossings only. They show when Nvidia's value reached each level, not whether it stayed above that level afterward. Round numbers such as $1 trillion are reference points for share-price valuation. They are not figures the company reported in its earnings.
